Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 12

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 12 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't flinch when the little fire stands up out of the floor. He watches it the way he'd watch a dog in the dark: not a threat yet, but not ignored.

"Right side is where the floor's breathing hardest," he says low. "We stay left on the swept path, tight to Ixx. Don't watch the bright cracks—watch where the next clean stone is." His voice is steady, but he has to work for it. The heat has a weight, and the air is like standing downwind of a forge with a bellows going.

He sets his boot exactly where the broom's bristles touch, then gives Ixx a nod. "Your floor, Ixx. Show us the next stone. And if one of those little fires comes out close, don't put yourself between it and me."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa stops at the mouth of the hall and lets her eyes go where the light goes — the bronze channels pierced like flutes, the glazed seam, the long black smear off the path with its two grooves running back toward the passage.

"Those channels aren't a dragon's work," she says, low, just for the two of them and Ixx. "Someone *built* this to carry heat — bronze pipes, vents, a floor laid over coals. Ulcaster's people, or whoever came after. The hot rooms were a working before they were a lair." Her eyes rest on the smear. "And that's where the four died. Off the path, by the bright cracks. Then dragged out — those are the grooves we saw on the stair."

She takes a breath of the flint-sharp air and doesn't like it. "Tisk said little fires walk here, small as Pikk's mother, and they know the Broom but may not know us. If something small and bright comes toward you, Bram, don't swing — step back onto a clean stone and let it pass. No iron in the red cracks, nothing stopped over them. Ixx's broom, then your foot, then Pell's, then mine, exactly."

To Ixx, gently: "You're doing well. Say the stone before Bram steps, not after."

Then she falls in last, hem gathered up out of the heat, hand resting on the Book of Shadows at her belt, and puts her boot only where Pell's has already been. As they go she keeps half an eye on the quiet left wall and the dimmer cracks — watching for anything that moves against the glow that isn't a flagstone breathing. *(Keeping to the swept path, passive Perception for the walking fires.)*

Pell Dunmore: “Little fire on the right,” I murmur to Sefa. “It looked toward the light, then sank back. Leaving it alone.”

I loosen the satchel flap so I can reach the potions, securing it against my hip. Then I follow Bram onto the stones Ixx marks, keeping my steps deliberate and my gear clear of the glowing cracks.

I watch for another flare or movement near our path. If I see one, I warn the others before taking the next step.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Ixx doesn't look at the place where the little fire went down. At Bram's words he moves the broom one stone forward and sets the bristles down.

"Ixx is not between," he says. "Ixx is behind." He thinks about it for a moment. "Behind is good."

Then the crossing starts, a stone at a time. Ixx calls each stone before Bram steps on it, the way Sefa asked. "Here." The broom touches down, Bram's boot follows, then Pell's, then Sefa's, each on the stone the last one just left. "Here." The path zigzags across the pale left side of the hall, sometimes two stones straight ahead, sometimes one stone sideways toward the wall. The flagstones are hot through Bram's boot soles, like the floor beside a smithy's hearth. The air above the cracks shimmers, and every breath of it scrapes the throat.

About a third of the way across, Ixx says "Wait" instead of "Here."

Bram stops with his weight on his back foot. Two stones ahead, where the path crosses a seam running out from the right side, the seam goes orange from one end to the other. It lets out a sigh of heat that pushes against their faces and throws sparks up past Bram's shield rim. Then it dulls back to red. Ixx counts something under his breath in his own tongue.

"Now. Fast. Here, here."

The broom taps twice and they cross the seam in two strides each, Sefa last. Behind her the seam is just a red line again.

Pell sees the second one first, and he says so before his next step, as he said he would. A crack off the path to the right, behind Sefa and well beyond the back of the light, brightens. Another little fire stands up out of it, knee-high, embers stacked into the shape of a hunched man. Out on the right side, about where the first one sank, the first one stands up again. Neither comes onto the path. They keep pace with the company stone by stone, each on its own line of cracks, staying out where Sefa's light gives way to red. When Sefa stops, the one behind her stops. When she steps, it steps. Once it drifts close enough that she can hear it, a dry crackle like green wood catching. It stops where the shield light thins to dim and goes no further. It sways there, and its two bright spots stay on her.

Ixx sees it too. Without a word he lifts the broom off the floor and tucks the whole bristle end behind his back, pole and all, the way a man tucks a full purse behind him in a crowd.

"They like broom," he says, very quietly. "Ixx likes broom more."

Halfway across, the path brings them level with the long black smear Sefa pointed out from the mouth of the hall. Up close, Bram reads it the way he'd read the ground after a wagon fight. The blackening is worst at one end and thins out toward the other. Something burned right there, and then it was dragged away while it was still burning: the twin grooves start at the thin end and run back toward the passage. Past the dark end, about fifteen feet off the path, a short bar of dark iron stands upright in a crack. It's as long as a man's forearm, with a flattened head like a mason's chisel, driven or wedged in hard. The crack around it is the brightest on that side of the hall, white-orange rather than red, and the bottom half of the bar has gone cherry-red with heat. Nobody here has iron in that crack except whoever left this. The little fire on the right sank back into the floor a few stones from it.

Ixx won't look at the bar. "Here," he says, and the path bends away from it toward the wall.

The rest of the way is the same work: the broom taps, a boot comes down, Ixx says "wait" once more and they wait. As the far end comes closer, both little fires fold down into their cracks, first the one on the right and then the one behind Sefa, and the floor goes back to being just a floor over coals. Ixx brings out his broom again and checks the bristles with one claw. None of them are scorched.

The arch at the far end is broad and low, built of the same dressed stone. Beyond it the deep, steady red light swells and fades with the breath that has come past them all afternoon. Here it's so close that they can feel each draw as a faint pull on their clothes and each letting-go as heat on their faces. Ixx stops one stone short of the arch, behind Bram's elbow, and his tail is round his ankle.

The breath stops.

Then a voice comes out of the red. It's deep enough that Bram feels it in his shield arm before he hears it, and slow, and in good, old-fashioned Common.

"The one who wonders what is so old it cannot climb its own stair." It pauses, and in the pause the heat leans on them. "Come and see, then, little shield. And bring the lamp nearer. I want to look at it."

*(Out of game: no dice this round. Ixx called every stone, the company stepped where he said, and anything moving against the glow was easy to see.)*
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
Sefa stops at the mouth of the hall, her eyes following the bronze channels, the glazed seam, and the long black smear off the path with its two grooves running back toward the passage. She speaks low, just to you, Pell and Ixx: "Those channels aren't a dragon's work. Someone *built* this to carry heat. Bronze pipes, vents, a floor laid over coals. Ulcaster's people, or whoever came after. The hot rooms were a working before they were a lair." She looks at the smear. "And that's where the four died. Off the path, by the bright cracks. Then dragged out. Those are the grooves we saw on the stair."

She breathes in the flint-sharp air and grimaces. "Tisk said little fires walk here, small as Pikk's mother, and they know the Broom but may not know us. If something small and bright comes toward you, Bram, don't swing. Step back onto a clean stone and let it pass. No iron in the red cracks, nothing stopped over them. Ixx's broom, then your foot, then Pell's, then mine, exactly." To Ixx, gently: "You're doing well. Say the stone before Bram steps, not after."

Pell murmurs something to Sefa and loosens the flap of his satchel, settling it against his hip. He falls in behind you, and Sefa takes the last place, hem gathered up out of the heat.

Ixx doesn't look at the place where the little fire went down. At your words he moves the broom one stone forward and sets the bristles down.

"Ixx is not between," he says. "Ixx is behind." He thinks about it for a moment. "Behind is good."

Then the crossing starts, a stone at a time. Ixx calls each stone before you step on it, the way Sefa asked. "Here." The broom touches down and your boot follows, then Pell's, then Sefa's, each on the stone the last one just left. "Here." The path zigzags across the pale left side of the hall. Sometimes it runs two stones straight ahead, sometimes one stone sideways toward the wall. The flagstones are hot through your boot soles, like the floor beside a smithy's hearth. The air above the cracks shimmers, and every breath of it scrapes your throat.

About a third of the way across, Ixx says "Wait" instead of "Here."

You stop with your weight on your back foot. Two stones ahead, where the path crosses a seam running out from the right side, the seam goes orange from one end to the other. It lets out a sigh of heat that pushes against your face and throws sparks up past your shield rim. Then it dulls back to red. Ixx counts something under his breath in his own tongue.

"Now. Fast. Here, here."

The broom taps twice and you all cross the seam in two strides each, Sefa last. Behind her the seam is just a red line again.

Pell calls it before his next step: a second little fire. A crack off the path to the right, behind Sefa and well beyond the back of the light, brightens. Another little fire stands up out of it, knee-high, embers stacked into the shape of a hunched man. Out on the right side, about where the first one sank, the first one stands up again. Neither comes onto the path. They keep pace with the company stone by stone, each on its own line of cracks, staying out where Sefa's light gives way to red. When Sefa stops, the one behind her stops. When she steps, it steps. Once it drifts closer to her and stops where the shield light thins to dim, and goes no further. It sways there, its two bright spots turned on her.

Ixx sees it too. Without a word he lifts the broom off the floor and tucks the whole bristle end behind his back, pole and all, the way a man tucks a full purse behind him in a crowd.

"They like broom," he says, very quietly. "Ixx likes broom more."

Halfway across, the path brings you level with the long black smear. Up close, you read it the way you'd read the ground after a wagon fight. The blackening is worst at one end and thins out toward the other. Something burned right there, and then it was dragged away while it was still burning: the twin grooves start at the thin end and run back toward the passage. Past the dark end, about fifteen feet off the path, a short bar of dark iron stands upright in a crack. It's as long as a man's forearm, with a flattened head like a mason's chisel, driven or wedged in hard. The crack around it is the brightest on that side of the hall, white-orange rather than red, and the bottom half of the bar has gone cherry-red with heat. Nobody in your company has put iron in that crack. Someone else left it. The little fire on the right sank back into the floor a few stones from it.

Ixx won't look at the bar. "Here," he says, and the path bends away from it toward the wall.

The rest of the way is the same work: the broom taps, your boot comes down, Ixx says "wait" once more and you wait. As the far end comes closer, both little fires fold down into their cracks, first the one on the right and then the one behind Sefa, and the floor goes back to being just a floor over coals. Ixx brings out his broom again and checks the bristles with one claw. None of them are scorched.

The arch at the far end is broad and low, built of the same dressed stone. Beyond it, the deep, steady red light swells and fades with the breath that has come past you all afternoon. Here it's so close that you feel each draw as a faint pull on your clothes and each letting-go as heat on your face. Ixx stops one stone short of the arch, behind your elbow, his tail wrapped round his ankle.

The breath stops.

Then a voice comes out of the red. It's deep enough that you feel it in your shield arm before you hear it, and slow, and in good, old-fashioned Common.

"The one who wonders what is so old it cannot climb its own stair." It pauses, and in the pause the heat leans on you. "Come and see, then, little shield. And bring the lamp nearer. I want to look at it."
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 13

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't answer the voice right away. He rolls his shoulders once, loosening them under the mail, and glances back at Ixx, then at Sefa and Pell, making sure they're all still on their feet and at their places.

"Steady," he says quietly, more to the company than to the voice. "It's older than it is subtle. It heard every word we said on the stair. We give it truth, not a show."

Then he steps through the arch with his shield up and the lamp-light spreading before him, not crowding the threshold. He keeps his sword hand easy at his belt, away from the hilt.

"I'm Bram Halloway." His voice carries clear through the heat. "We came down your stair talking, not with night-quiet and iron. The lamp's not mine to offer—she who holds it speaks for it. But it's here."

He stops where the floor lets him see the shape of the thing: what it lies on, how it fills the room, where its head is, whether it moves to breathe or the room moves with it.

"You asked us to come see. We're here. Now I'll ask what you wanted us to see—the door, or you. And what you'd have us do about either."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa stops at Bram's shoulder — beside the shield, not past it — and does not take the light off the rim. She looks at the arch and the red breathing through it, and lets the heat lean on her for a moment before she answers.

"The lamp stays where it is. It's lighting the floor, and I've been told not to stop over the cracks." Her voice is the ward voice still, unhurried, pitched to carry into the red without rising. "You can look at it from there as well as I can look at you. I'm Sefa Marrin. Bram you've heard. The quiet one is Pell. We came down talking, as your kobold asked us to, and we tended a child on the way, because he was coughing on what comes up out of your floor." A beat. "Which is not your breath. I know what dragonfire smells like from the books, and this isn't it. This is called fire — a working. So before you tell me anything, I'd like to hear you say that much true, and then I'll believe the rest of it more easily."

She tips her head back toward the hall, without looking away from the arch. "And there's an iron bar standing in the brightest crack out there, by the place the four died. Tisk's people won't look at it. I'd like to know who put it in — them, or you."

Low, barely moving her lips, to Bram and Pell: "He speaks like someone who learned Common from a book, not a kobold. Educated. Let him come to the arch; we don't cross it till we've seen the shape of him." Her hand rests on the Book of Shadows, and her thumb finds the beads at her throat.

Pell Dunmore: I keep my hands clear of my weapons and stay behind Bram, leaving Ixx room beside him.

“Pell Dunmore,” I say into the red. “We’ve come to hear about your door.”

I wait for Ixx to mark the footing through the arch before following. As the chamber comes into view, I look for a safe place where we can stand together without blocking our way back.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram steps through the arch, and the shield-light goes through with him.

The room beyond is big, the biggest thing they've seen under the hill. It's round, about sixty feet across, with a dome high enough to lose the shield-light in. The floor is dressed stone and it's whole here, with no cracks between the flags. It's hot, steady and dry, the way the air over a hearth is hot. The arch they came through is worn smooth on both sides at the height of a horse's shoulder, polished pale. Something big has rubbed past it many times.

The red light comes from the far wall, from a ring.

A channel runs around a round slab set into the wall, about twelve feet across. It's a door made of pale stone, banded with bronze gone black. The channel glows the same coal-red as the cracks in the hot rooms, and it breathes. Above the door the tower is cut into the stone, Ulcaster's stepped crown, sharper than the one on the lintel. In the middle of the slab is the lantern, hooded, with a ring for a handle, the same as at the mouth. In its heart, where the carving would hold a flame, a small light burns. It is lamp-yellow and no bigger than one of Sefa's beads. It doesn't flicker. On the bronze rim, low on the left, a bright scar shows where something has been cut away, leaving pale stone under the black.

Between the arch and the door lies the Warm One.

Bram does what he came in to do and reads the shape. It's his trade, the same as judging a team at a distance. The dragon lies along the floor in a long curve, with his tail toward the right-hand wall and well clear of the arch. His body is as thick as a loaded cart and longer than a wagon with its team. Under him there's a bed of metal: coins, the rim of a cup, a bent candlestick, old gold pressed flat under his weight. His head rests on his crossed forelegs a few strides from the door, turned toward that small yellow flame, and it's the size of a rain barrel. His wings are folded tight along his back. Their edges are ragged and dull, rubbed thin, the way a sail goes when it's been stowed wet too long. The scales should be red, and they are, the deep red of a forge coal, but they're dusty and dry, gone pale round the nostrils and along the jaw. His flanks rise slowly. Bram counts the rise against the warm pull on his clothes. The breath of this whole ruin, the one that's been coming up past them since the grass at the mouth, matches the dragon's breathing exactly.

The head lifts and turns. The eyes are pale gold. They go first to the shield-light and stay there for a long moment. Then, without the head moving, they slide past it to the yellow bead in the door, and they stay on that even longer. Then they come back to Bram.

"Bram Halloway." He says the name the way a man turns a coin over to read its date. "*Truth, not a show.* You said that at my threshold, as if the stone between us were deaf." His breath stops. In the stillness the heat presses on all of them, on the face and the chest. Then it comes out again, slow and warmer. "Then have it. You wondered what is so old it cannot climb its own stair. I am not old, little man. I have had twenty winters in this house. I came down that stair on my own feet when I was smaller than your shield. I stay because what I want is here."

His snout tips a hand's width toward the door.

"That is what I wanted you to see. Your wizards shut it, and their light keeps it shut, and their light is going out. You have eyes. What I would have you do about it I will tell you when I know what you are worth."

Sefa comes to Bram's shoulder and stops in the arch itself, under its worn stones, beside the shield. She says her piece into the heat. Her hand is on the Book of Shadows at her belt, and under her palm the pale leather is warmer than the room accounts for. It's warm like a hand held, not like a stone in the sun.

The gold eyes move to her at *the lamp stays where it is*, and the pause after it is long.

"Then I will look from here." He does, and not at the lamp. He looks past it at the door again, and then at her. "*Called fire. A working.* You read well, for a sick-house girl." A low sound moves through the floor. It could be amusement. "Yes. That is the house you smell, not me. If you ever smell my breath, you will not be standing to discuss it."

At the iron bar the long head settles back onto the forelegs.

"Your kind put it there. Four of them, by night, with cold iron, cutting at the house's veins to get at my door. The house answered them." The eyes half close. "Had I answered them, the Broom would have found nothing to carry up."

Behind them, Ixx stops at the arch and sets his broom-tip on the first flag inside.

"Here floor is floor," he whispers. "Not near the ring. Not near the shine." Then he stays exactly where he is, one stone back, with his tail round his ankle.

Pell steps through after Bram's boots, keeping his hands clear and saying his name into the red. He picks a place the way he would pick a spot in a room full of people he doesn't trust. Just inside the arch on the left, the wall curves out into a stub of old pillar. Standing there, he has stone at his back and the arch within two strides, and he's the farthest point in the room from the tail. There's room in that space for three, close together. From there he can see the head, the door and the way out, all at once.

"Pell Dunmore," the Warm One repeats, without looking at him. "The quiet one, who came to *hear about my door*." The gold eyes stay on Bram. "Well. You came down talking. Talk. What does the man with the shield want for the use of his hands?"

*(Out of game: Bram is one stride inside the chamber, and Sefa stands in the arch beside his shield, not yet over the threshold. No dice were needed this round.)*
````

````text
"Then I will look from here." He does, and not at the lamp. He looks past it at the door again, and then at her. "*Called fire. A working.* You read well, for a sick-house girl." A low sound moves through the floor. It could be amusement. "Yes. That is the house you smell, not me. If you ever smell my breath, you will not be standing to discuss it."

At the iron bar the long head settles back onto the forelegs.

"Your kind put it there. Four of them, by night, with cold iron, cutting at the house's veins to get at my door. The house answered them." The eyes half close. "Had I answered them, the Broom would have found nothing to carry up."

Behind you, Ixx stops at the arch and sets his broom-tip on the first flag inside.

"Here floor is floor," he whispers. "Not near the ring. Not near the shine." Then he stays exactly where he is, one stone back, with his tail round his ankle.

Pell steps through after your boots, keeping his hands clear of his weapons. "Pell Dunmore," he says into the red. "We've come to hear about your door." He moves just inside the arch on the left, where the wall curves out into a stub of old pillar. It's the farthest point in the room from the tail, the arch two strides from it, with room there for three standing close.

"Pell Dunmore," the Warm One repeats, without looking at him. "The quiet one, who came to *hear about my door*." The gold eyes stay on you. "Well. You came down talking. Talk. What does the man with the shield want for the use of his hands?"

*(Out of game: you are one stride inside the chamber, and Sefa stands in the arch beside your shield, not yet over the threshold. No dice were needed this round.)*
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram doesn't answer the voice right away. He rolls his shoulders once under the mail and glances back at Ixx, then at you and Pell.

"Steady," he says quietly. "It's older than it is subtle. It heard every word we said on the stair. We give it truth, not a show."

Then he steps through the arch with his shield up, the shield-light spreading before him, not crowding the threshold. His sword hand stays easy at his belt, away from the hilt.

"I'm Bram Halloway." His voice carries clear through the heat. "We came down your stair talking, not with night-quiet and iron. The lamp's not mine to offer—she who holds it speaks for it. But it's here." He stops a stride inside. "You asked us to come see. We're here. Now I'll ask what you wanted us to see—the door, or you. And what you'd have us do about either."

The room beyond is big, the biggest thing you've seen under the hill. It's round, about sixty feet across, with a dome high enough to lose the shield-light in. The floor is dressed stone and it's whole here, with no cracks between the flags. It's hot, steady and dry, the way the air over a hearth is hot. The arch is worn smooth on both sides at the height of a horse's shoulder, polished pale. Something big has rubbed past it many times.

The red light comes from the far wall, from a ring.

A channel runs around a round slab set into the wall, about twelve feet across. It's a door made of pale stone, banded with bronze gone black. The channel glows the same coal-red as the cracks in the hot rooms, and it breathes. Above the door the tower is cut into the stone, Ulcaster's stepped crown, sharper than the one on the lintel. In the middle of the slab is the lantern, hooded, with a ring for a handle, the same as at the mouth. In its heart, where the carving would hold a flame, a small light burns. It is lamp-yellow and no bigger than one of your beads. It doesn't flicker. On the bronze rim, low on the left, a bright scar shows where something has been cut away, leaving pale stone under the black.

Between the arch and the door lies the Warm One.

The dragon lies along the floor in a long curve, with his tail toward the right-hand wall and well clear of the arch. His body is as thick as a loaded cart and longer than a wagon with its team. Under him there's a bed of metal: coins, the rim of a cup, a bent candlestick, old gold pressed flat under his weight. His head rests on his crossed forelegs a few strides from the door, turned toward that small yellow flame, and it's the size of a rain barrel. His wings are folded tight along his back. Their edges are ragged and dull, rubbed thin, the way a sail goes when it's been stowed wet too long. The scales are the deep red of a forge coal, but they're dusty and dry, gone pale round the nostrils and along the jaw. His flanks rise slowly.

The head lifts and turns. The eyes are pale gold. They go first to the shield-light and stay there for a long moment. Then, without the head moving, they slide past it to the yellow bead in the door, and they stay on that even longer. Then they come back to Bram.

"Bram Halloway." He says the name the way a man turns a coin over to read its date. "*Truth, not a show.* You said that at my threshold, as if the stone between us were deaf." His breath stops. In the stillness the heat presses on all of you, on the face and the chest. Then it comes out again, slow and warmer. "Then have it. You wondered what is so old it cannot climb its own stair. I am not old, little man. I have had twenty winters in this house. I came down that stair on my own feet when I was smaller than your shield. I stay because what I want is here."

His snout tips a hand's width toward the door.

"That is what I wanted you to see. Your wizards shut it, and their light keeps it shut, and their light is going out. You have eyes. What I would have you do about it I will tell you when I know what you are worth."

You come to Bram's shoulder and stop in the arch itself, under its worn stones, beside the shield, and say your piece into the heat. Your hand is on the Book of Shadows at your belt, and under your palm the pale leather is warmer than the room accounts for. It's warm like a hand held, not like a stone in the sun.

The gold eyes move to you at *the lamp stays where it is*, and the pause after it is long.

"Then I will look from here." He does, and not at the lamp. He looks past it at the door again, and then at you. "*Called fire. A working.* You read well, for a sick-house girl." A low sound moves through the floor. It could be amusement. "Yes. That is the house you smell, not me. If you ever smell my breath, you will not be standing to discuss it."

At the iron bar the long head settles back onto the forelegs.

"Your kind put it there. Four of them, by night, with cold iron, cutting at the house's veins to get at my door. The house answered them." The eyes half close. "Had I answered them, the Broom would have found nothing to carry up."

Behind you, Ixx stops at the arch and sets his broom-tip on the first flag inside.

"Here floor is floor," he whispers. "Not near the ring. Not near the shine." Then he stays exactly where he is, one stone back, with his tail round his ankle.

Pell steps through after Bram's boots, hands clear of his weapons. "Pell Dunmore," he says into the red. "We've come to hear about your door." He moves just inside the arch on the left, where the wall curves out into a stub of old pillar. It's the farthest point in the room from the tail, the arch two strides from it, with room there for three standing close.

"Pell Dunmore," the Warm One repeats, without looking at him. "The quiet one, who came to *hear about my door*." The gold eyes stay on Bram. "Well. You came down talking. Talk. What does the man with the shield want for the use of his hands?"

*(Out of game: Bram is one stride inside the chamber, and you stand in the arch beside his shield, not yet over the threshold. No dice were needed this round.)*
````

I know what dragonfire smells like from the books, and this isn't it. This is called fire — a working. So before you tell me anything, I'd like to hear you say that much true, and then I'll believe the rest of it more easily."

````text
She tips her head back toward the hall without looking away. "And there's an iron bar standing in the brightest crack out there, by the place the four died. Tisk's people won't look at it. I'd like to know who put it in — them, or you."
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 14

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram meets the gold eyes and doesn't step any closer.

"My hands aren't for sale until I know what they're buying." His voice stays level, the same tone he'd use to price a run through bandit country. "You've shown me a door and a failing light. That's a problem, not a bargain."

He plants the shield a little more solidly against the heat.

"What I want, in order:

"First, I want the Broom out of this without your heel on them. Ixx brought us down in daylight with straight answers, and your own word got flung at them because we stopped to tend a sick child. I don't work for anyone who treats good service with a threat. So I'll have that said plainly: they stay, and we don't find them worse after we leave.

"Second, I'll want to know what's behind that door, as true as you know it. Not the name, if you don't have it. But what it is, what it does when it's open, and whether the thing that's failing is meant to hold it or let it loose. Because I read the floor too. That heat under the cracks is no natural coal. It'll bite, and it'll take the one who steps wrong.

"Third, my price. You can think on that while I look at the door and hear the rest of what you know. I don't work on a 'may it be enough' promise, and I don't open old seals for old gold alone.

"Last, I want this company to have free passage back up the stair while I think, with our own feet and our own steel, and no clever floor between us and the sky."

He pauses, then adds, not unkindly: "You want hands that won't fail you at the wrong moment. That means you want the one that asks all this. We came down talking. Let's keep doing it."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa's hand stays on the Book. The leather is warm as a held hand under her palm, and she doesn't look down at it; she keeps her eyes on the gold ones.

"Bram will tell you what he wants for his hands. I'll tell you what mine cost, and it isn't gold." She doesn't step over the threshold. "You've answered me straight twice — about the fire, and about the four. So I'll ask the third thing straight. You came down a stair smaller than a shield, following the warm, twenty winters ago. You've grown to fill the house. Now you lie here watching the wizards' light die on that door, and you say *what I want is here*." A beat. "What's behind it? Not 'the fire-not-fire' — Tisk gave me that much. What do you think is on the other side that's worth twenty years on a bed of other men's cups, and what do you become when it opens?"

Her thumb moves on the beads. "Because here's what I see from the arch. The lintel upstairs says *by lent light*. That flame in the lantern is the same colour as the one in my hands, and it's been going out all Tisk's life, and the floor breathes hotter every year it fades. That isn't a lock, Warm One. That's a *lid*. Somebody lent a light to hold a fire down, and the loan is running out. If that door opens, I don't think what comes through is a way up for you. I think it's the house, getting out."

She tips her head at the bronze rim, low on the left, where the pale stone shows through the black. "And someone has already cut a piece off it. Was that the four, or was that you, in a year when you were less patient?"

Low, to Bram and Pell, barely moving her lips: "He hasn't said what the door *does*. He's said what he wants. Don't promise hands until he tells us which way it opens."

Pell Dunmore: I stay by the pillar, hands relaxed and empty.

“Bram names his own price. I deal in locks—but I need to know what the job is before I can price mine.”

I glance toward the pale scar on the bronze rim without moving closer.

“Do you want that door opened, or its workings repaired? What’s behind it that you want—and what was cut from the rim there?”

“No tools on the stone until we’ve agreed. Looking first costs you nothing.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The Warm One hears Bram's list to the end without moving. When Bram says *threat*, the gold eyes narrow, and they stay narrowed until he has finished.

"In order, then, since you like it that way."

"The Broom. I said they would go up with you, and so they would. A broom goes where it is carried." The sound that moves through the floor might be a laugh. "I do not break my own brooms, Bram Halloway. Who would sweep? Have it plainly, then. They stay. When you go, you will find them as you found them, sweeping."

Behind Bram's elbow, Ixx's tail tightens once around his ankle.

"Your passage. Go up whenever you please." The great head doesn't move. "Nobody going up that stair has ever been stopped."

Then Sefa speaks, and he turns his eyes to her. At *a bed of other men's cups* his breath stops. The heat leans on all of them and keeps leaning, longer than it has before, until it presses on the chest like a hand. Along his flank the tail shifts on the hoard, and a slide of old coin runs off the bed and rings across the stone. Then he breathes out, slow and warmer.

"*Other men's cups.*" He gives it back to her softly. "Every cup in this room belonged to a dead man before it belonged to me, sick-house girl. That is what gold is."

"What lies behind it. The fire your wizards caught and could never put down, and the thing they caught it with: a book bound in iron. The four talked about it on my floor, loudly, as though I were furniture. They wanted the book. I have no use for paper." The eyes half close. "And what do I become? What I am."

"A lid. Yes. You read well." For a moment he is quiet. "Think what happens under a lid that is lifted. The fire rushes out, burns, and when it has nothing left to eat it is less than it was. Now think what happens under a lid that cracks by itself, slowly, a little more every year." He lets the hot rooms answer for him. Out beyond the arch a seam sighs orange and goes dull. "You have felt that floor. When it comes, it will not trouble me. You will be up the stair long before it reaches the hot rooms. I would see to that. Dead hands are no use to me twice."

His snout tips toward the pale scar on the bronze rim.

"The four did that. They had a cold-iron tool and a thief's patience. They cut at the rim, and I let them, because I wanted to see what the door would do." The gold eyes go to the yellow bead in the lantern. "The light jumped when the iron bit. They ran as though it had bitten them back, and they took the piece with them. The house caught them on the floor. When the Broom carried them up, nobody had it. They put it down somewhere between my door and the stair."

He turns to the pillar, and to Pell.

"Opened, little lock. Not mended. You mend what you mean to keep."

The long head comes up off the forelegs then, high enough that the shield-light finally reaches his throat. The scales there are as dull as old brick.

"My price, then, since you asked for yours in order. Open it, and each of you takes from this bed as much as you can carry in two arms, and the iron book goes with you." His breath stops again, briefly. "But tall ones have shown me their backs before. So one of you stays here beside me while the other two go and find what was cut from my door."

The gold eyes come back to Bram and stay on him.

"The man with the shield. At the mouth of my passage you said *I'll take the red walk first.* You are the one who stands where the trouble will come. So come and stand here."

*(Out of game: nobody needed a die. All three of you asked your questions aloud, and he answered them. Whether he told the truth is something the story will show.)*
````

````text
The Warm One hears Bram's list to the end without moving. When Bram says *threat*, the gold eyes narrow, and they stay narrowed until he has finished.

"In order, then, since you like it that way."

"The Broom. I said they would go up with you, and so they would. A broom goes where it is carried." The sound that moves through the floor might be a laugh. "I do not break my own brooms, Bram Halloway. Who would sweep? Have it plainly, then. They stay. When you go, you will find them as you found them, sweeping."

Behind Bram's elbow, Ixx's tail tightens once around his ankle.

"Your passage. Go up whenever you please." The great head doesn't move. "Nobody going up that stair has ever been stopped."

Then you speak, and he turns his eyes to you. At *a bed of other men's cups* his breath stops. The heat leans on all of you and keeps leaning, longer than it has before, until it presses on the chest like a hand. Along his flank the tail shifts on the hoard, and a slide of old coin runs off the bed and rings across the stone. Then he breathes out, slow and warmer.

"*Other men's cups.*" He gives it back to you softly. "Every cup in this room belonged to a dead man before it belonged to me, sick-house girl. That is what gold is."

"What lies behind it. The fire your wizards caught and could never put down, and the thing they caught it with: a book bound in iron. The four talked about it on my floor, loudly, as though I were furniture. They wanted the book. I have no use for paper." The eyes half close. "And what do I become? What I am."

"A lid. Yes. You read well." For a moment he is quiet. "Think what happens under a lid that is lifted. The fire rushes out, burns, and when it has nothing left to eat it is less than it was. Now think what happens under a lid that cracks by itself, slowly, a little more every year." He lets the hot rooms answer for him. Out beyond the arch a seam sighs orange and goes dull. "You have felt that floor. When it comes, it will not trouble me. You will be up the stair long before it reaches the hot rooms. I would see to that. Dead hands are no use to me twice."

His snout tips toward the pale scar on the bronze rim.

"The four did that. They had a cold-iron tool and a thief's patience. They cut at the rim, and I let them, because I wanted to see what the door would do." The gold eyes go to the yellow bead in the lantern. "The light jumped when the iron bit. They ran as though it had bitten them back, and they took the piece with them. The house caught them on the floor. When the Broom carried them up, nobody had it. They put it down somewhere between my door and the stair."

He turns to the pillar, and to Pell.

"Opened, little lock. Not mended. You mend what you mean to keep."

The long head comes up off the forelegs then, high enough that the shield-light finally reaches his throat. The scales there are as dull as old brick.

"My price, then, since you asked for yours in order. Open it, and each of you takes from this bed as much as you can carry in two arms, and the iron book goes with you." His breath stops again, briefly. "But tall ones have shown me their backs before. So one of you stays here beside me while the other two go and find what was cut from my door."

The gold eyes come back to Bram and stay on him.

"The man with the shield. At the mouth of my passage you said *I'll take the red walk first.* You are the one who stands where the trouble will come. So come and stand here."

*(Out of game: nobody needed a die. All three of you asked your questions aloud, and he answered them. Whether he told the truth is something the story will show.)*
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram meets the gold eyes and doesn't step any closer.

"My hands aren't for sale until I know what they're buying." His voice stays level. "You've shown me a door and a failing light. That's a problem, not a bargain."

He plants the shield a little more solidly against the heat.

"What I want, in order:

"First, I want the Broom out of this without your heel on them. Ixx brought us down in daylight with straight answers, and your own word got flung at them because we stopped to tend a sick child. I don't work for anyone who treats good service with a threat. So I'll have that said plainly: they stay, and we don't find them worse after we leave.

"Second, I'll want to know what's behind that door, as true as you know it. Not the name, if you don't have it. But what it is, what it does when it's open, and whether the thing that's failing is meant to hold it or let it loose. Because I read the floor too. That heat under the cracks is no natural coal. It'll bite, and it'll take the one who steps wrong.

"Third, my price. You can think on that while I look at the door and hear the rest of what you know. I don't work on a 'may it be enough' promise, and I don't open old seals for old gold alone.

"Last, I want this company to have free passage back up the stair while I think, with our own feet and our own steel, and no clever floor between us and the sky."

He pauses, then adds, not unkindly: "You want hands that won't fail you at the wrong moment. That means you want the one that asks all this. We came down talking. Let's keep doing it."

Sefa keeps her hand on the Book and her eyes on the gold ones. She doesn't step over the threshold.

"Bram will tell you what he wants for his hands. I'll tell you what mine cost, and it isn't gold. You've answered me straight twice — about the fire, and about the four. So I'll ask the third thing straight. You came down a stair smaller than a shield, following the warm, twenty winters ago. You've grown to fill the house. Now you lie here watching the wizards' light die on that door, and you say *what I want is here*." A beat. "What's behind it? Not 'the fire-not-fire' — Tisk gave me that much. What do you think is on the other side that's worth twenty years on a bed of other men's cups, and what do you become when it opens?"

Her thumb moves on her beads. "Because here's what I see from the arch. The lintel upstairs says *by lent light*. That flame in the lantern is the same colour as the one in my hands, and it's been going out all Tisk's life, and the floor breathes hotter every year it fades. That isn't a lock, Warm One. That's a *lid*. Somebody lent a light to hold a fire down, and the loan is running out. If that door opens, I don't think what comes through is a way up for you. I think it's the house, getting out."

She tips her head at the bronze rim, low on the left, where pale stone shows through the black. "And someone has already cut a piece off it. Was that the four, or was that you, in a year when you were less patient?"

Low, to you and Bram, barely moving her lips: "He hasn't said what the door *does*. He's said what he wants. Don't promise hands until he tells us which way it opens."

Along his flank the tail shifts on the hoard, and a slide of old coin runs off the bed and rings across the stone. Then he breathes out, slow and warmer.

"*Other men's cups.*" He gives it back to her softly. "Every cup in this room belonged to a dead man before it belonged to me, sick-house girl. That is what gold is."

"What lies behind it. The fire your wizards caught and could never put down, and the thing they caught it with: a book bound in iron. The four talked about it on my floor, loudly, as though I were furniture. They wanted the book. I have no use for paper." The eyes half close. "And what do I become? What I am."

"A lid. Yes. You read well." For a moment he is quiet. "Think what happens under a lid that is lifted. The fire rushes out, burns, and when it has nothing left to eat it is less than it was. Now think what happens under a lid that cracks by itself, slowly, a little more every year." He lets the hot rooms answer for him. Out beyond the arch a seam sighs orange and goes dull. Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]
````

## Round 15

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't move from his one stride inside the room, but he doesn't flinch either. The shield stays up, the lamp-light throwing his shadow long toward the sealed door.

"Here's my difficulty," he says after the dragon's last word. "You want me to stand where the trouble will come, off the path, beside a lid that's been cracking for years, while the other two go looking for a piece of cold iron and a book. That doesn't sound like a bargain. It sounds like the four who ran and got caught—except you'd be holding their shieldman when the floor gets hungry."

He shakes his head slowly, the mail shifting with the breath still pressing the room.

"I don't send my people out without me, and I don't stand at the seal with my back to it while you and the fire decide who's worth more. So here's my counter-offer, and it's the one I'll keep."

He counts it on his left hand, two fingers, shield bound to his arm.

"One: the piece from the door gets found before anyone touches that slab. I'll search for it, or Pell will, or the Broom's kin who know the hot rooms better than we do—but it gets found and brought into this room, where we all see it. If it touches that ring or the door, we stop, and we talk again.

"Two: nobody stays beside you as surety. We all stay outside the arch, or all inside where you can see us, but always the three of us together. If we need to cross the hot rooms, we go and come back by the same swept path. You want hands? You get hands that trust each other, not a man left waiting like a hostage.

"Three: the iron book goes to the door before any of us begins. I'll not send someone six steps down a wizard's vault with no more than a promise about what they'll find. You say you have no use for paper—then show us the book, or show us your word that it's there. If the door opening is part of the work, we'll see it before the first pry."

He lets his hand drop to his belt. His stare stays level with the gold eyes.

"I don't doubt you know this house better than any man alive. But I'll walk out of it the same way I came: with my people, and my shield, and my own word for the price."

Then, very quietly to Sefa and Pell, but pitched so the dragon hears: "Say no, and we go up. The Broom comes with us. I don't want to leave Pikk to a hotter floor."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa lets the coin finish ringing across the floor before she speaks. She hasn't moved off the threshold, and her hand hasn't left the Book; under her palm it is warmer still.

"No hostage. Not Bram, not Pell, not me." She says it quietly, without heat, the way she'd refuse a patient a second draught. "You've just told me what a cracked lid does to this house, and in the same breath that *dead hands are no use to you twice*. Those are the words of someone counting how many of us he needs alive. I'm not leaving one of us on your bed to make the arithmetic easier." She glances at Bram. "It's your name he called. But I'd not go, and I'd not let a sister go, if that's worth anything."

"Here's what I'll offer instead, and it's more than you asked. The piece lies between your door and the stair — Ixx's people know every stone of that path, and we'll have it found before dark. That part we'll do, because a cut rim is a cracked lid whether anyone ever opens it or not." A breath. "But the opening — that, I haven't agreed to, and I'll tell you why to your face. A man with a cold-iron chisel bit that rim and *the light jumped*. You've lain three strides from that door for twenty winters with claws that could shear the bronze, and you've never once put them on it. You need tall hands because the door won't let *you* near it. Say I'm wrong."

She tips her head at the little yellow bead. "And you still haven't told me what you become. *What I am* is a riddle, not an answer. A red wyrm that fire can't trouble, sitting in a house that's about to burn its way up to the grass — I can guess what that looks like from Beregost."

Then, more carefully, because this is the part she actually wants: "Let me go to the door. Alone, no iron, no tools, the lamp stays on the shield. I can read what's written on it — all of it, whatever hand it's in — and the light in that lantern is the same colour as the one that answered me in the dark. I'd like to see what it does when I stand in front of it. I think you'd like to see that too. If the four's words are to be believed, there's an iron book on the other side that *caught* this fire once. You want it gone. I want to know whether it can do the catching again — because *mend* is the only word in this room that keeps the Broom alive."

Low, barely moving her lips, to Bram and Pell: "He can't touch it. That's the whole of why we're here. Pell — while he's looking at me, look at the scar on the rim and the floor between here and the arch; the piece is small enough that a dying man dropped it. Bram, you don't answer him about standing anywhere until I've seen that door."

Pell Dunmore: “I can look for the missing piece. That isn’t a promise to open the door.”

I keep my voice businesslike. “But not with Bram held as surety. You’ve just offered us free passage; let that stand. We came down willingly, and Ixx can guide us back willingly. If your offer is worth taking, you don’t need a hostage.”

I glance at the scar again. “Before anyone moves: why do you need the cut piece for opening it? Does it carry part of the mechanism, or writing you need read?”

Quietly to my companions: “I’d recover it and learn what it does. No pledge beyond that—and I’d keep us together.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The Warm One hears all of it without moving: Bram's counting on two fingers, Sefa's refusal, Pell's question. His gold eyes go from one speaker to the next. Only his breath changes. While Bram talks it stays slow. At *the four who ran and got caught* it stops, then starts again.

At Sefa's *say I'm wrong*, it stops and doesn't start again.

The heat builds in the silence. It presses on their faces, then their chests, then the backs of their eyes, until Bram's mail is hot through its padding and the sweat dries on Pell's neck before it can run. Then, for the first time since they came in, the dragon gets up.

He takes his time over it. His forelegs straighten and his head rises above the light and keeps rising, past Bram's height and past the top of the arch, until the shield-light only reaches his chest. His folded wings shift along his back with a sound like old canvas, and his tail drags across the hoard. Cups and coins go sliding and ringing over the stone, and a bent candlestick rolls nearly to Pell's boots. Inside the throat above them, behind the dull brick-red scales, a light shows. It's a slow orange glow that rises up the length of his neck like a coal being blown on, and it reaches the hinge of his jaw.

Ixx, one stone back, makes a small sound and folds himself down behind Bram's leg with his broom flat on the floor.

The glow sits at the dragon's jaw for one long breath, and then it sinks back down his throat and goes out. He lowers himself again, without hurry, until his head is level with them a few strides away. He breathes out, and the breath is very hot.

"*Say I'm wrong.*" He gives it back to Sefa quietly. "I said you read well, sick-house girl. Read the door, then. Do not read me." He doesn't say she's wrong.

He turns to Bram.

"Your three things. Keep your three together. I can watch three as easily as one, and I am tired of hearing about the floor." His eyes narrow. "The book is behind the door, little shield. That is what a door is for. You will see it when it opens and not before, and no wish of yours changes that."

"The Broom." The word comes out low, from deep in his chest. "I told you they stay. They are mine. You will not carry my brooms up my stair because you are sorry for a coughing egg." The gold eyes hold Bram's. "Go up when you like. Go up with nothing, and you go up as you came. Go up with what is mine, and you will find out what that stair has never stopped."

Then, to the pillar, almost lazily: "*Why do you need the cut piece.* It was cut from a lock, little lock. You tell me what a piece of a lock is good for." He doesn't explain further.

He turns back to Sefa, and then he moves.

He swings his great head aside, to the left and down, and lays his jaw on the stone along the line of his own foreleg. That opens a lane of bare floor from the threshold straight to the pale round door. It's about fifteen strides long and a little wider than a cart. Along its whole left side lies the dragon's head, close enough to touch, and his half-closed eye follows the lane. Beyond the end of the lane the coal-red ring breathes around the slab, and in the carved lantern the yellow bead burns steadily.

"*Let me go to the door. Alone.*" His voice is soft now. "That is the first wise thing a tall one has said in my house. Go and stand in front of it, and show me what it does when it sees you." His breath goes out, slow and even. "Then we will talk about mending, if you still want to."

Behind Bram's leg, Ixx says something too quiet for the dragon to catch, and maybe meant for nobody. "Not near the ring." His tail is wound twice around his ankle.

Under Sefa's palm the Book of Shadows is as warm as a hand holding hers. The lane lies open in front of her, and she's still on the threshold.

 *(Out of game: none of the three needed a roll, and all three happened as you declared them. Bram held his place one stride inside the room with his shield up, and he never stepped into the lane toward the door. His counter-offer was speech, and the Warm One answered it point by point: three can stay together, the book stays behind the door until it opens, and the Broom stays below.)*
````

Cups and coins go sliding and ringing over the stone, and a bent candlestick rolls nearly to Pell's boots. Inside the throat above you, behind the dull brick-red scales, a light shows. It's a slow orange glow that rises up the length of his neck like a coal being blown on, and it reaches the hinge of his jaw.

````text
Ixx, one stone back, makes a small sound and folds himself down behind Bram's leg with his broom flat on the floor.

The glow sits at the dragon's jaw for one long breath, and then it sinks back down his throat and goes out. He lowers himself again, without hurry, until his head is level with you a few strides away. He breathes out, and the breath is very hot.

"*Say I'm wrong.*" He gives it back to you quietly. "I said you read well, sick-house girl. Read the door, then. Do not read me." He doesn't say you're wrong.

He turns to Bram.

"Your three things. Keep your three together. I can watch three as easily as one, and I am tired of hearing about the floor." His eyes narrow. "The book is behind the door, little shield. That is what a door is for. You will see it when it opens and not before, and no wish of yours changes that."

"The Broom." The word comes out low, from deep in his chest. "I told you they stay. They are mine. You will not carry my brooms up my stair because you are sorry for a coughing egg." The gold eyes hold Bram's. "Go up when you like. Go up with nothing, and you go up as you came. Go up with what is mine, and you will find out what that stair has never stopped."

Then, to Pell by the pillar, almost lazily: "*Why do you need the cut piece.* It was cut from a lock, little lock. You tell me what a piece of a lock is good for." He doesn't explain further.

He turns back to you, and then he moves.

He swings his great head aside, to the left and down, and lays his jaw on the stone along the line of his own foreleg. That opens a lane of bare floor from the threshold straight to the pale round door. It's about fifteen strides long and a little wider than a cart. Along its whole left side lies the dragon's head, close enough to touch, and his half-closed eye follows the lane. Beyond the end of the lane the coal-red ring breathes around the slab, and in the carved lantern the yellow bead burns steadily.

"*Let me go to the door. Alone.*" His voice is soft now. "That is the first wise thing a tall one has said in my house. Go and stand in front of it, and show me what it does when it sees you." His breath goes out, slow and even. "Then we will talk about mending, if you still want to."

Behind Bram's leg, Ixx's tail is wound twice around his ankle.

Under your palm the Book of Shadows is as warm as a hand holding yours. The lane lies open in front of you, and you're still on the threshold.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram doesn't move from his one stride inside the room, but he doesn't flinch either. The shield stays up, the lamp-light throwing his shadow long toward the sealed door.

"Here's my difficulty," he says after the dragon's last word. "You want me to stand where the trouble will come, off the path, beside a lid that's been cracking for years, while the other two go looking for a piece of cold iron and a book. That doesn't sound like a bargain. It sounds like the four who ran and got caught, except you'd be holding their shieldman when the floor gets hungry."

He shakes his head slowly, the mail shifting.

"I don't send my people out without me, and I don't stand at the seal with my back to it while you and the fire decide who's worth more. So here's my counter-offer, and it's the one I'll keep."

He counts it on his left hand, two fingers, shield bound to his arm.

"One: the piece from the door gets found before anyone touches that slab. I'll search for it, or Pell will, or the Broom's kin who know the hot rooms better than we do, but it gets found and brought into this room, where we all see it. If it touches that ring or the door, we stop, and we talk again.

"Two: nobody stays beside you as surety. We all stay outside the arch, or all inside where you can see us, but always the three of us together. If we need to cross the hot rooms, we go and come back by the same swept path. You want hands? You get hands that trust each other, not a man left waiting like a hostage.

"Three: the iron book goes to the door before any of us begins. I'll not send someone six steps down a wizard's vault with no more than a promise about what they'll find. You say you have no use for paper, then show us the book, or show us your word that it's there. If the door opening is part of the work, we'll see it before the first pry."

He lets his hand drop to his belt. His stare stays level with the gold eyes.

"I don't doubt you know this house better than any man alive. But I'll walk out of it the same way I came: with my people, and my shield, and my own word for the price."

Then, very quietly to you and Sefa, but pitched so the dragon hears: "Say no, and we go up. The Broom comes with us. I don't want to leave Pikk to a hotter floor."

Sefa lets the coin finish ringing across the floor before she speaks. She hasn't moved off the threshold, and her hand hasn't left the Book.

"No hostage. Not Bram, not Pell, not me." She says it quietly, without heat. "You've just told me what a cracked lid does to this house, and in the same breath that *dead hands are no use to you twice*. Those are the words of someone counting how many of us he needs alive. I'm not leaving one of us on your bed to make the arithmetic easier." She glances at Bram. "It's your name he called. But I'd not go, and I'd not let a sister go, if that's worth anything."

"Here's what I'll offer instead, and it's more than you asked. The piece lies between your door and the stair. Ixx's people know every stone of that path, and we'll have it found before dark. That part we'll do, because a cut rim is a cracked lid whether anyone ever opens it or not." A breath. "But the opening, that I haven't agreed to, and I'll tell you why to your face. A man with a cold-iron chisel bit that rim and *the light jumped*. You've lain three strides from that door for twenty winters with claws that could shear the bronze, and you've never once put them on it. You need tall hands because the door won't let *you* near it. Say I'm wrong."

She tips her head at the little yellow bead. "And you still haven't told me what you become. *What I am* is a riddle, not an answer. A red wyrm that fire can't trouble, sitting in a house that's about to burn its way up to the grass. I can guess what that looks like from Beregost."

Then, more carefully: "Let me go to the door. Alone, no iron, no tools, the lamp stays on the shield. I can read what's written on it, all of it, whatever hand it's in, and the light in that lantern is the same colour as the one that answered me in the dark. I'd like to see what it does when I stand in front of it. I think you'd like to see that too. If the four's words are to be believed, there's an iron book on the other side that *caught* this fire once. You want it gone. I want to know whether it can do the catching again, because *mend* is the only word in this room that keeps the Broom alive."

Low, barely moving her lips, to you and Bram: "He can't touch it. That's the whole of why we're here. Pell, while he's looking at me, look at the scar on the rim and the floor between here and the arch; the piece is small enough that a dying man dropped it. Bram, you don't answer him about standing anywhere until I've seen that door."

The Warm One hears all of it without moving. His gold eyes go from one speaker to the next, and only his breath changes. While Bram talks it stays slow. At *the four who ran and got caught* it stops, then starts again.

At Sefa's *say I'm wrong*, it stops and doesn't start again.

The heat builds in the silence. It presses on your face, then your chest, then the backs of your eyes, until the sweat dries on your neck before it can run. Then, for the first time since you came in, the dragon gets up.

He takes his time over it. His forelegs straighten and his head rises above the light and keeps rising, past Bram's height and past the top of the arch, until the shield-light only reaches his chest. His folded wings shift along his back with a sound like old canvas, and his tail drags across the hoard. Cups and coins go sliding and ringing over the stone, and a bent candlestick rolls nearly to your boots. Inside the throat above you, behind the dull brick-red scales, a light shows. It's a slow orange glow that rises up the length of his neck like a coal being blown on, and it reaches the hinge of his jaw.

Ixx, one stone back, makes a small sound and folds himself down behind Bram's leg with his broom flat on the floor.

The glow sits at the dragon's jaw for one long breath, and then it sinks back down his throat and goes out. He lowers himself again, without hurry, until his head is level with you a few strides away. He breathes out, and the breath is very hot.

"*Say I'm wrong.*" He gives it back to Sefa quietly. "I said you read well, sick-house girl. Read the door, then. Do not read me." He doesn't say she's wrong.

He turns to Bram.

"Your three things. Keep your three together. I can watch three as easily as one, and I am tired of hearing about the floor." His eyes narrow. "The book is behind the door, little shield. That is what a door is for. You will see it when it opens and not before, and no wish of yours changes that."

"The Broom." The word comes out low, from deep in his chest. "I told you they stay. They are mine. You will not carry my brooms up my stair because you are sorry for a coughing egg." The gold eyes hold Bram's. "Go up when you like. Go up with nothing, and you go up as you came. Go up with what is mine, and you will find out what that stair has never stopped."

Then, to you by the pillar, almost lazily: "*Why do you need the cut piece.* It was cut from a lock, little lock. You tell me what a piece of a lock is good for." He doesn't explain further.

He turns back to Sefa, and then he moves.

He swings his great head aside, to the left and down, and lays his jaw on the stone along the line of his own foreleg. That opens a lane of bare floor from the threshold straight to the pale round door. It's about fifteen strides long and a little wider than a cart. Along its whole left side lies the dragon's head, close enough to touch, and his half-closed eye follows the lane. Beyond the end of the lane the coal-red ring breathes around the slab, and in the carved lantern the yellow bead burns steadily.

"*Let me go to the door. Alone.*" His voice is soft now. "That is the first wise thing a tall one has said in my house. Go and stand in front of it, and show me what it does when it sees you." His breath goes out, slow and even. "Then we will talk about mending, if you still want to."

Behind Bram's leg, Ixx's tail is wound twice around his ankle.

The lane lies open in front of Sefa, and she's still on the threshold.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 16

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't step into the lane. He turns his head just enough to catch Sefa's eye, then looks toward the door, then back to her.

"Fifteen strides, no tools, no iron, lamp stays with me." He says it like a road order, calm and final. "You go no further than the first bronze band. If the light in that lantern so much as flickers, you step back the same way—no turning your back on it, no jumping. Keep your hands where we can see them."

He plants his shield edge on the stone and shifts his weight forward just enough that the dragon can see he's ready, though his sword stays sheathed.

"Pell—watch the ring, not her back. I'll watch his jaw and the lane. If the floor breathes wrong, I call and we all move. Back to the arch, then the hot path exactly as Ixx showed us."

Then he looks up at the great head lying along the lane.

"She goes alone, as you asked. Not because you asked—because she's earned the right to read a wizard's door before a soldier puts his hands on it." His voice is low and measured. "But make no play at her while she's on that floor. I've seen a tethered ox take a man's arm off for less than a twitch at his hand." He pauses. "And I'm not tethered."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa looks down the lane — fifteen strides of bare stone, the dragon's jaw lying the length of it like a wall, the half-closed gold eye at her elbow, the little yellow bead burning steady at the far end. Then she unbuckles the dagger from her belt and holds it out to Bram, hilt first, without looking away from the door.

"Hold this. I said no iron, and I meant it for his door as much as for Hrask's small ones." Quiet, for the two of them: "If the light does something I don't like, I've a step that can carry me back to this arch faster than anything in this room can turn its head. Don't come after me. Watch the floor between here and the hall — Pell, you heard him: a *piece of a lock*. It was cut from the rim, it's small, and four frightened men dropped it running. The light jumped when iron bit the door. Mind that." A glance at the kobold folded behind Bram's leg. "And don't let Ixx go back across that hall alone."

To the Warm One, as she steps over the threshold: "I'll read the door. Don't mistake me, though — I've already read enough of you to know you'd burn the Broom to keep them, and call it owning. I'll remember that when we talk about mending."

Then she walks the lane. Slowly, hands open and a little away from her sides, the way she'd cross a ward at night so as to wake no one. The heat from the ring grows with every stride; she breathes through her mouth and doesn't hurry. She does not touch the slab. Two paces short of it she stops, where the red breath of the channel is on her face, and does three things in order.

First she looks. Not at the lantern yet — at the stone around it, the bronze bands, the pale scar low on the left, every groove and line the dome-light and the ring-glow pick out. Whatever is written there, in whatever hand, she reads it, and reads it twice before she lets herself think about what it means. *(Eyes of the Rune Keeper.)*

Then she looks at the flame. The bead of yellow in the carved lantern — whether it holds steady, whether it leans toward her the way a candle leans toward a draught, whether it does anything at all now that she's standing in front of it.

Last, without closing the distance, she lifts her right hand and turns the palm toward the lantern — not touching, a hand's width of hot air between skin and stone — and lets the Book at her belt be as warm as it wants to be. She says nothing aloud. In her head, the way she did on the ninth night with the oil gone: *If this is yours, show me. I'm listening.*

Pell Dunmore: I leave the candlestick where it rolled and keep my hands empty.

Quietly to Sefa: “I’ll look from here. You need us, call.”

Without leaving the pillar or approaching the hoard, I study the scar on the rim: its shape, any exposed fittings, and whether the missing section carried part of an inscription. Then I scan the bare floor back toward the arch for a matching fragment or scrape marks. I’m looking only—not touching the door or anything on the floor.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram's orders are short, and the room is quiet enough that they carry. The gold eye on the lane doesn't leave Sefa while he gives them. When he turns to the great head and says *tethered ox*, the dragon's breath stops.

This time it stays stopped. The heat in the chamber gathers and holds, and the coins under the near foreleg tick and settle as the claws flex into them, slowly, the way a man's fingers close on a table edge. Bram is watching the jaw, as he said he would, and he sees the lips draw back a finger's width off teeth as long as his hand. Then they close again.

"*I'm not tethered,*" the Warm One says quietly. He doesn't turn his head. "No. You are not." The breath comes out, long and very hot, and that's all he answers. His eye goes back to the woman at the head of the lane. Bram has seen this on the road. A man who has a better use for the next minute puts a quarrel in his pocket. He doesn't throw it away.

Sefa holds her dagger out to Bram, hilt first, then steps off the threshold onto bare stone.

She says her piece about the Broom to the dragon as she passes his jaw. The eye half closes and doesn't open again until she has gone three strides on. He lets that one go into the same pocket.

The lane is fifteen strides long. On her left the whole way lies his head and then his neck. The scales are dull and dusty, and close enough that she could lay a hand on them. Each breath he lets out moves her hair. The heat doubles, then doubles again, until the air in her mouth tastes of struck flint and old coins. Two paces short of the slab she stops, with the ring's red breath on her face and the first bronze band at the height of her eyes.

The door is written on. She would have missed it from the arch. Up close it is everywhere, a single line of old Chondathan worked into the blackened bronze of the outer band. It starts at the crown under Ulcaster's tower and runs clockwise around the whole slab. The sight her pact gave her takes it in the way her eyes take in a face, and she reads it twice.

*By lent light the last of the house of Ulcaster shut the Cellar, in the year the towers burned. Come in who will. Nothing of the flame goes out.*

*The light is lent by*

Then the band stops. It hasn't worn away. It has been cut, low on the left, at the bright scar. A piece about a hand long has gone, and the letters on either side are sliced through the middle. Past the gap the line picks up again:

*—and it is owed. When the flame is small as a seed, the one who carries it shall come to this door, and the light shall go home.*

That's all of it. Whatever stood between *lent by* and *and it is owed* went up the stair ten days ago in a frightened man's pocket.

Then she looks at the flame. It sits in the carved lantern's heart, a bead of yellow that doesn't flicker, the same steady lamp-colour as the light on Bram's shield behind her. She lifts her right hand and turns the palm toward it, a hand's width short of the stone, and says nothing aloud.

The flame leans.

It leans the way a candle leans toward an opened door, toward her palm. For the length of a breath it is bigger than a bead, the size of a cherry stone, and its light falls warm across her fingers. The coal-red ring around the slab misses a breath. The channel should brighten, and it stays dull. Then it comes back. At her belt the Book of Shadows is hot, hotter than a held hand. It's the heat of a cup just filled, and she can feel it through the leather and through her coat.

Behind her, the Warm One's breath stops again. The sound that comes through the floor this time isn't amusement.

Pell is at the pillar, forty-odd feet from the door, and he reads the rim the way he would read a strongbox from across a counting-room. The outer band isn't one piece. In the ring-glow he can count the seams where cast plates meet, each about a hand long, pinned at the joins by bronze rivets with their heads ground flush. He counts nine of them around the circle. When the flame leans toward Sefa's hand, a hairline of yellow runs along every seam at once, like light under a row of doors, and is gone. It runs along eight seams. At the ninth place, low on the left, there is no plate to run behind. There's only the bright cut and pale stone, and a thin channel in the stone where the plate sat, red as the ring. He has seen this shape before, on a treasury lock in the Upper City: a ward ring with a plate drawn. Somebody cut that plate out between its two rivets, carefully, along the seam, and they knew what they were doing right up to the moment they ran.

He sweeps the floor from the pillar back to the arch. The stone is whole and swept by the dragon's own bulk. Coins have scattered where the tail dragged, the candlestick lies by his boot, and he can see the polished stone where something huge has turned around ten thousand times. There's no brass on it, no plate, no scuff of boots running. Whatever the four carried out of here, they didn't drop it in this room.

The Warm One lifts his head off the stone. He keeps it low, level with Sefa, so close that the heat of it lies on her cheek.

"It knows you," he says. He says it softly, and he means it. "Twenty winters I have lain here, and it has never once leaned for me."

He lets that sit while the heat leans on her.

"You read the gap, sick-house girl. I read the band before the little thieves cut it. I read it every sleep for twenty winters, until I could say it with my eyes shut. I know what stood there." The gold eye is a stride from her face. "The name your Lantern was called by the ones who borrowed from it, long before it came to a dark ward. And what they promised it, the size of the lending, which is the size of what you owe. Open my door, and I will give you both."

The flame in the lantern flares.

It doesn't lean this time. It *flares*. It's white-gold and sudden, and it throws the carved hood's shadow huge across the slab and lights the dragon's dull scales. It lights Bram at the arch, and Pell at his pillar, and Ixx pressed flat behind Bram's boot with his snout on the stone. For an instant the whole room is the colour of the light in Sefa's hands on the ninth night.

At the very edge of her hearing, so close it could be inside her own ear, a voice like a lamp being lit says:

*"That light is mine. Take it back."*

Then the flame is a bead again. The ring breathes red. The Warm One hasn't moved, and his eye hasn't left her face.

"Well?" he says.

*(Out of game: no dice this turn. Sefa's dagger is held out to Bram, but his paragraph doesn't say he takes it, so I've left it on the threshold stone at his boot. If Bram's player says he took it, it's in his hand.)*

 *(Out of game: none of the four needed a die, and each one was played in the turn:)*

1. **Bram's orders and stance.** Giving orders, planting the shield and keeping his sword sheathed carry no uncertainty, so they happened as declared. The Warm One heard every word and took the "tethered ox" line without acting.
2. **Sefa's dagger.** She held it out hilt first, and that happened. Bram's paragraph doesn't say he takes it, so the dagger lies on the threshold stone at his boot. If Bram's player says he took it, it's in his hand.
3. **Walking the lane and reading the door.** The walk was open, slow and unopposed. Eyes of the Rune Keeper reads all writing without a check. The inscription is given word for word, up to the cut gap.
4. **The raised palm.** As Sefa's player wrote it, this was a silent prayer with no touch, not an attempt to work the lock, so there was nothing to roll. The flame leaning toward her hand was the world answering her.
````
