````text
*By lent light the last of the house of Ulcaster shut the Cellar, in the year the towers burned. Come in who will. Nothing of the flame goes out.*

*The light is lent by*

Then the band stops. It hasn't worn away. It has been cut, low on the left, at the bright scar. A piece about a hand long has gone, and the letters on either side are sliced through the middle. Past the gap the line picks up again:

*—and it is owed. When the flame is small as a seed, the one who carries it shall come to this door, and the light shall go home.*

That's all of it. Whatever stood between *lent by* and *and it is owed* went up the stair ten days ago in a frightened man's pocket.

Then you look at the flame. It sits in the carved lantern's heart, a bead of yellow that doesn't flicker, the same steady lamp-colour as the light on Bram's shield behind you. You lift your right hand and turn the palm toward it, a hand's width short of the stone.

The flame leans.

It leans toward your palm the way a candle leans toward an opened door. For the length of a breath it is bigger than a bead, the size of a cherry stone, and its light falls warm across your fingers. The coal-red ring around the slab misses a breath. The channel should brighten, and it stays dull. Then it comes back. At your belt the Book of Shadows is hot, hotter than a held hand. It's the heat of a cup just filled, and you can feel it through the leather and through your coat.

Behind you, the Warm One's breath stops again. The sound that comes through the floor this time isn't amusement.

He lifts his head off the stone. He keeps it low, level with you, so close that the heat of it lies on your cheek.

"It knows you," he says. He says it softly, and he means it. "Twenty winters I have lain here, and it has never once leaned for me."

He lets that sit while the heat leans on you.

"You read the gap, sick-house girl. I read the band before the little thieves cut it. I read it every sleep for twenty winters, until I could say it with my eyes shut. I know what stood there." The gold eye is a stride from your face. "The name your Lantern was called by the ones who borrowed from it, long before it came to a dark ward. And what they promised it, the size of the lending, which is the size of what you owe. Open my door, and I will give you both."

The flame in the lantern flares.

It doesn't lean this time. It *flares*, white-gold and sudden. It throws the carved hood's shadow huge across the slab and lights the dragon's dull scales. It lights Bram at the arch, and Pell at his pillar, and Ixx pressed flat behind Bram's boot with his snout on the stone. For an instant the whole room is the colour of the light in your hands on the ninth night.

At the very edge of your hearing, so close it could be inside your own ear, a voice like a lamp being lit says:

*"That light is mine. Take it back."*

Then the flame is a bead again. The ring breathes red. The Warm One hasn't moved, and his eye hasn't left your face.

"Well?" he says.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram doesn't step into the lane. He catches Sefa's eye, looks toward the door, then back to her.

"Fifteen strides, no tools, no iron, lamp stays with me." He says it like a road order, calm and final. "You go no further than the first bronze band. If the light in that lantern so much as flickers, you step back the same way. No turning your back on it, no jumping. Keep your hands where we can see them."

He plants his shield edge on the stone and shifts his weight forward, sword still sheathed.

"Pell, watch the ring, not her back. I'll watch his jaw and the lane. If the floor breathes wrong, I call and we all move. Back to the arch, then the hot path exactly as Ixx showed us."

Then he looks up at the great head lying along the lane. "She goes alone, as you asked. Not because you asked. Because she's earned the right to read a wizard's door before a soldier puts his hands on it." His voice is low and measured. "But make no play at her while she's on that floor. I've seen a tethered ox take a man's arm off for less than a twitch at his hand." He pauses. "And I'm not tethered."

The gold eye on the lane doesn't leave Sefa while he gives his orders. When he says *tethered ox*, the dragon's breath stops.

This time it stays stopped. The heat in the chamber gathers and holds. The coins under the near foreleg tick and settle as the claws flex into them, slowly, the way a man's fingers close on a table edge.

"*I'm not tethered,*" the Warm One says quietly. He doesn't turn his head. "No. You are not." The breath comes out, long and very hot, and that's all he answers. His eye goes back to the woman at the head of the lane.

Sefa unbuckles her dagger and holds it out to Bram hilt first, without looking away from the door. "Hold this. I said no iron, and I meant it for his door as much as for Hrask's small ones." Quietly, for you and Bram: "If the light does something I don't like, I've a step that can carry me back to this arch faster than anything in this room can turn its head. Don't come after me. Watch the floor between here and the hall. Pell, you heard him: a *piece of a lock*. It was cut from the rim, it's small, and four frightened men dropped it running. The light jumped when iron bit the door. Mind that." She glances at Ixx behind Bram's leg. "And don't let Ixx go back across that hall alone."

The dagger ends up on the threshold stone at Bram's boot as she steps off onto bare stone. As she passes the dragon's jaw she says: "I'll read the door. Don't mistake me, though. I've already read enough of you to know you'd burn the Broom to keep them, and call it owning. I'll remember that when we talk about mending." The eye half closes and doesn't open again until she has gone three strides on.

She walks the lane slowly, hands open and a little away from her sides, his head and then his neck lying along her left the whole way. Two paces short of the slab she stops in the red breath of the ring and stands a long while looking at the door, without touching it.

You are at the pillar, forty-odd feet from the door, and you read the rim the way you would read a strongbox from across a counting-room. The outer band isn't one piece. In the ring-glow you can count the seams where cast plates meet, each about a hand long, pinned at the joins by bronze rivets with their heads ground flush. You count nine of them around the circle.

Sefa looks at the flame in the carved lantern, a steady bead of yellow. She lifts her right hand and turns the palm toward it, a hand's width short of the stone, and says nothing.

The flame leans toward her palm, the way a candle leans toward an opened door. For the length of a breath it is bigger than a bead. The coal-red ring around the slab misses a breath and stays dull where it should brighten, then comes back. As the flame leans, a hairline of yellow runs along every seam at once, like light under a row of doors, and is gone. It runs along eight seams. At the ninth place, low on the left, there is no plate to run behind. There's only the bright cut and pale stone, and a thin channel in the stone where the plate sat, red as the ring. You have seen this shape before, on a treasury lock in the Upper City: a ward ring with a plate drawn. Somebody cut that plate out between its two rivets, carefully, along the seam, and they knew what they were doing right up to the moment they ran.

You sweep the floor from the pillar back to the arch. The stone is whole and swept by the dragon's own bulk. Coins have scattered where the tail dragged, the candlestick lies by your boot, and you can see the polished stone where something huge has turned around ten thousand times. There's no brass on it, no plate, no scuff of boots running. Whatever the four carried out of here, they didn't drop it in this room.

The Warm One's breath stops again. The sound that comes through the floor this time isn't amusement.

He lifts his head off the stone and keeps it low, level with Sefa and very close to her.

"It knows you," he says. He says it softly, and he means it. "Twenty winters I have lain here, and it has never once leaned for me."

He lets that sit.

"You read the gap, sick-house girl. I read the band before the little thieves cut it. I read it every sleep for twenty winters, until I could say it with my eyes shut. I know what stood there." The gold eye is a stride from her face. "The name your Lantern was called by the ones who borrowed from it, long before it came to a dark ward. And what they promised it, the size of the lending, which is the size of what you owe. Open my door, and I will give you both."

The flame in the lantern flares.

It doesn't lean this time. It *flares*, white-gold and sudden. It throws the carved hood's shadow huge across the slab and lights the dragon's dull scales. It lights Bram at the arch, and you at your pillar, and Ixx pressed flat behind Bram's boot with his snout on the stone. For an instant the whole room is lit in white-gold.

Then the flame is a bead again. The ring breathes red. The Warm One hasn't moved, and his eye hasn't left Sefa's face.

"Well?" he says.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 17

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 17 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't move when the flare fills the room, though his shield comes up a few inches by reflex. He watches the flame die back to a bead, and then he steps forward one pace—not into the lane, just into the room, making sure the dragon can see him clearly again.

"Sefa," he says, calm, like calling a horse in a storm. "Come back to the arch. Nice and slow."

Then, to the Warm One, his voice hard but not raised:

"You lay that in front of her like a sugar lump on a mousetrap. We haven't agreed to open anything. And you keep saying *open my door* as if the only thing between us and that sky is a hinge." His grip on the shield stays loose. "If that light flared on its own, then it knows the word you almost spoke. You'd have killed half the room just to see whether it would."

He lets that hang, then jerks his chin toward the door.

"Your price is information. Fine. But we're not at the door yet, not while we don't know what's behind it, and not while the piece of rim is still lying somewhere in your house. Sefa reads. Pell searches. I stand. That's the work we do." He glances back at Pell, just once, as if checking he's still there, then back.

"Show me the lantern's true face. Not a flare—prove you want this thing opened slow and not by the first fool with a crowbar. Then we find your cut piece together, and you stop bidding for my people's debts like they're coins."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa doesn't answer at once. She lowers her hand, slowly, and takes one pace back from the slab — exactly one, the way Bram told her to, with her eyes still on the bead of yellow and not on the dragon's eye at her cheek. The Book at her belt is hot as a full cup against her hip, and she lets it be.

*I heard you,* she tells it, in the place where she talked to it on the ninth night. *I'll bring you home. I'll find the way that doesn't burn twenty small bodies in a goat-smoke cellar to do it. You waited a hundred years. You can wait till dark.*

Then, aloud, to the gold eye a stride from her face, and the ward voice doesn't shake, though it costs her something:

"It leaned for me. You saw it; I'll not pretend otherwise." A breath of flint-hot air. "And you just offered to sell me a thing that's lying on your own floor. The name, and the size of the lending — they're on a hand's length of bronze that four frightened men dropped between here and the stair. You've known that for ten days and never sent a kobold to pick it up, because a piece of a lock is no use to you without hands, and because as long as it's lost you've something to bargain with." She lets that stand. "I'll not be bought with my own debt, Warm One. I'll go and read it off the bronze myself, with my own eyes, and owe you nothing for it."

She tips her head at the outer band without touching it. "And I'll tell you what *I* read that you've never said in twenty winters of reading it with your eyes shut. *Come in who will. Nothing of the flame goes out.* That's not a lock, and it's not only a lid. It's a door a person can pass with the fire still held. The last of Ulcaster's house meant someone to walk in there and come out again — and leave the fire behind them. I think that's how the iron book gets *mended*, and I think you know it, and I think it's the one thing in this room you don't want."

She turns, then — not her back to the door, side-on, the way you'd leave a sickbed where the patient might wake — and raises her voice just enough to carry down the lane.

"Bram. Pell. The band says the house of Ulcaster shut this cellar *by lent light*, the year the towers burned. The light was lent by — and there the piece is cut — and it is owed. When the flame is small as a seed, the one who carries it comes to this door and the light goes home." Her hand rests on the Book. "That's me. It's been coming to me for six years and I didn't know what it was for." Quieter, as she starts back along the lane, slow, hands open: "The flare was it answering. I'll tell you what it said at the arch, not here. First the piece. Before anyone's hand goes near this door, I want to read whose name I've been carrying — and whether what's written after *owed* says anything about what happens to the house when the light leaves it."

At Ixx, still flat behind Bram's boot, she says gently as she comes level: "Tisk's people know every clean stone between here and the stair. When we go back across, I want someone who knows which cracks a dropped bit of bronze would have fallen near — and which ones it would have fallen *into*."

And to the dragon, over her shoulder, not stopping: "You'll have your answer. It's *not yet.* If that's not good enough for a thing that's waited twenty winters, say so now, while I'm still on your floor and you can still see my hands."

Pell Dunmore: “The missing piece is a ward plate,” I say, keeping my voice steady. “Cut out between two rivets. Eight seams lit when Sefa raised her hand; the gap stayed red. I see no plate on the floor here.”

I stay by the pillar, watching the ring rather than reaching for anything.

“Sefa, we’ve tracks into the burned room upstairs, about ten days old. That’s somewhere we can look without touching this seal.”

I leave the dragon’s offer for her to answer.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The light shrinks back to a bead. For a moment after it does, nobody in the room moves except Bram. He takes one pace into the chamber, clear of the lane, with his shield a few inches higher than it was, and calls Sefa back in the voice he'd use for a horse in a storm.

The Warm One doesn't look at him. The gold eye stays on the woman two paces from his door, and when Bram's words reach him (*sugar lump*, *mousetrap*, *killed half the room*) his breath stops and stays stopped. The heat gathers around them.

Sefa lowers her hand and steps back from the slab. It's one pace, exactly the one she was told to take, and her eyes stay on the bead. Then she answers him. She says her piece about being bought with her own debt, about the bronze lying somewhere on his floor, about *nothing of the flame goes out*. Then she turns side-on and starts back along the lane, slowly, hands open, with fifteen strides of his jaw on her left.

The long head turns to follow her. It doesn't come any closer. It only turns, the way a cat's head turns after a moth.

When he breathes out at last, it is slow, and hotter than anything yet. The coins under his foreleg tick in it.

"That was not mine." He says it to Bram, without moving his eyes off Sefa. "Twenty winters that light has not answered me. You saw what it did for her. Do not tell me I made it." He lets a pause go by. "*Prove you want it opened slow.* I have lain here twenty winters, Bram Halloway. Show me a slower man than that."

Sefa is a third of the way down the lane. He speaks to her now, low enough that the stone under their boots carries it more than the air does.

"*Never sent a kobold.*" He gives the words back to her almost gently. "Ask the one behind your shield-man how many times my floor was swept for it, sick-house girl. Ask him how many times the stair was swept, and the hot rooms, and the passage, crack by crack. Ask him what they found." The eye half closes. "You read stone better than you read me."

She is halfway now, level with the hinge of his jaw. When she's in reach of it, he takes the other thing she said and turns it in front of her.

"*Nothing of the flame goes out.* You say it as though it frightened you." The heat leans on her again, and this time it lies on her cheek. "Then think what it promises. If nothing of the flame goes out, what do you fear from an open door? The fire stays where your wizards put it. It says so in bronze. You read it to me yourself."

He doesn't say what he is. He doesn't say what *he* is made of.

At the pillar Pell speaks of plates and rivets and the burned room upstairs. For the first time the head moves away from Sefa. It's only a hand's width, toward him.

"The burned room." He tastes the words. "Your four went in there by night with their lamps before they came to me. My brooms do not go in." The amusement comes up through the floor, faint and not kind. "They are small, and they are afraid of old paper. Go and look, little lock. Look anywhere you like."

Sefa reaches the end of the lane. Her dagger is still on the threshold stone at Bram's boot, where she held it out to him. Ixx is still flat behind that boot with his snout on the floor. As she comes level he turns his head without lifting it, and whispers so faintly that it's barely a word:

"Swept all. Many sleeps. He said *find.*" His tail is wound twice round his ankle. "Broom found nothing." Then, even more faintly: "Not in the burned room. Broom does not go there."

The Warm One lowers his jaw onto his forelegs again, facing the door, as though none of them had ever come in.

"*Not yet,*" he says to the yellow bead. "I have had *not yet* from that door every sleep for twenty winters. I can hear it once more from you." He doesn't raise his voice. "Go and find my piece. Take the sweeper. Read your name off it with your own eyes, since you would rather owe me nothing. But it is a piece of *my* door, and it comes back to this room in your hands. If I hear it go up my stair instead…" A slide of old coin runs off the bed and rings across the stone, without hurry. "…then I will not need sweepers anymore, and the Broom will learn what I do with things I do not need."

His breath goes out, long and even, and the ruin breathes with it.

"One sleep. Not two."

*(Out of game: no dice this round. Everything you three declared happens, and Sefa is back at the arch beside Bram.)*
````

````text
You reach the end of the lane. Your dagger is still on the threshold stone at Bram's boot, where you held it out to him. Ixx is still flat behind that boot with his snout on the floor. As you come level he turns his head without lifting it and whispers, so faintly that it's barely a word:

"Swept all. Many sleeps. He said *find.*" His tail is wound twice round his ankle. "Broom found nothing." Then, even more faintly: "Not in the burned room. Broom does not go there."

The Warm One lowers his jaw onto his forelegs again, facing the door, as though none of you had ever come in.

"*Not yet,*" he says to the yellow bead. "I have had *not yet* from that door every sleep for twenty winters. I can hear it once more from you." He doesn't raise his voice. "Go and find my piece. Take the sweeper. Read your name off it with your own eyes, since you would rather owe me nothing. But it is a piece of *my* door, and it comes back to this room in your hands. If I hear it go up my stair instead…" A slide of old coin runs off the bed and rings across the stone, without hurry. "…then I will not need sweepers anymore, and the Broom will learn what I do with things I do not need."

His breath goes out, long and even, and the ruin breathes with it.

"One sleep. Not two."

You are back at the arch beside Bram.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
When the flare fills the room, Bram doesn't move, though his shield comes up a few inches by reflex. He watches the flame die back to a bead. Then he steps forward one pace, not into the lane, just into the room, where the dragon can see him clearly again.

"Sefa," he says, calm, the way he'd call a horse in a storm. "Come back to the arch. Nice and slow."

Then, to the Warm One, his voice hard but not raised:

"You lay that in front of her like a sugar lump on a mousetrap. We haven't agreed to open anything. And you keep saying *open my door* as if the only thing between us and that sky is a hinge." His grip on the shield stays loose. "If that light flared on its own, then it knows the word you almost spoke. You'd have killed half the room just to see whether it would."

He lets that hang, then jerks his chin toward the door.

"Your price is information. Fine. But we're not at the door yet, not while we don't know what's behind it, and not while the piece of rim is still lying somewhere in your house. Sefa reads. Pell searches. I stand. That's the work we do." He glances back at you once, then away again. "Show me the lantern's true face. Not a flare — prove you want this thing opened slow and not by the first fool with a crowbar. Then we find your cut piece together, and you stop bidding for my people's debts like they're coins."

Sefa lowers her hand, slowly, and takes exactly one pace back from the slab, her eyes on the bead of yellow and not on the dragon's eye at her cheek. Then, aloud, to the gold eye a stride from her face, her voice steady:

"It leaned for me. You saw it; I'll not pretend otherwise. And you just offered to sell me a thing that's lying on your own floor. The name, and the size of the lending — they're on a hand's length of bronze that four frightened men dropped between here and the stair. You've known that for ten days and never sent a kobold to pick it up, because a piece of a lock is no use to you without hands, and because as long as it's lost you've something to bargain with." She lets that stand. "I'll not be bought with my own debt, Warm One. I'll go and read it off the bronze myself, with my own eyes, and owe you nothing for it."

She tips her head at the outer band without touching it. "And I'll tell you what *I* read that you've never said in twenty winters of reading it with your eyes shut. *Come in who will. Nothing of the flame goes out.* That's not a lock, and it's not only a lid. It's a door a person can pass with the fire still held. The last of Ulcaster's house meant someone to walk in there and come out again — and leave the fire behind them. I think that's how the iron book gets *mended*, and I think you know it, and I think it's the one thing in this room you don't want."

She turns side-on to the door, not giving it her back, and raises her voice enough to carry down the lane.

"Bram. Pell. The band says the house of Ulcaster shut this cellar *by lent light*, the year the towers burned. The light was lent by — and there the piece is cut — and it is owed. When the flame is small as a seed, the one who carries it comes to this door and the light goes home." Her hand rests on the Book at her belt. "That's me. It's been coming to me for six years and I didn't know what it was for." Quieter, as she starts back along the lane, slowly, hands open: "The flare was it answering. I'll tell you what it said at the arch, not here. First the piece. Before anyone's hand goes near this door, I want to read whose name I've been carrying — and whether what's written after *owed* says anything about what happens to the house when the light leaves it."

As she comes level with Bram, she says gently to Ixx, still flat behind Bram's boot: "Tisk's people know every clean stone between here and the stair. When we go back across, I want someone who knows which cracks a dropped bit of bronze would have fallen near — and which ones it would have fallen *into*."

And to the dragon, over her shoulder, without stopping: "You'll have your answer. It's *not yet.* If that's not good enough for a thing that's waited twenty winters, say so now, while I'm still on your floor and you can still see my hands."

---

The light shrinks back to a bead. For a moment after it does, nobody in the room moves except Bram, who takes one pace into the chamber, clear of the lane, his shield a few inches higher than it was.

The Warm One doesn't look at him. The gold eye stays on Sefa, two paces from his door. When Bram's words reach him (*sugar lump*, *mousetrap*, *killed half the room*) his breath stops and stays stopped, and the heat gathers around them.

As Sefa starts back along the lane, with fifteen strides of his jaw on her left, the long head turns to follow her. It comes no closer. It only turns, the way a cat's head turns after a moth.

When he breathes out at last, it is slow, and hotter than anything yet. The coins under his foreleg tick in it.

"That was not mine." He says it to Bram, without moving his eyes off Sefa. "Twenty winters that light has not answered me. You saw what it did for her. Do not tell me I made it." He lets a pause go by. "*Prove you want it opened slow.* I have lain here twenty winters, Bram Halloway. Show me a slower man than that."

Sefa is a third of the way down the lane. He speaks to her now, low enough that the stone under your boots carries it more than the air does.

"*Never sent a kobold.*" He gives the words back to her almost gently. "Ask the one behind your shield-man how many times my floor was swept for it, sick-house girl. Ask him how many times the stair was swept, and the hot rooms, and the passage, crack by crack. Ask him what they found." The eye half closes. "You read stone better than you read me."

She is halfway now, level with the hinge of his jaw. With her in reach of it, he takes the other thing she said and turns it in front of her.

"*Nothing of the flame goes out.* You say it as though it frightened you." The heat leans on her again. "Then think what it promises. If nothing of the flame goes out, what do you fear from an open door? The fire stays where your wizards put it. It says so in bronze. You read it to me yourself."

He doesn't say what he is.

When you speak of plates and rivets and the burned room upstairs, the head moves away from Sefa for the first time. It's only a hand's width, toward you.

"The burned room." He tastes the words. "Your four went in there by night with their lamps before they came to me. My brooms do not go in." Amusement comes up through the floor, faint and not kind. "They are small, and they are afraid of old paper. Go and look, little lock. Look anywhere you like."

Sefa reaches the end of the lane. Her dagger is still on the threshold stone at Bram's boot. Ixx is still flat behind that boot with his snout on the floor and his tail wound twice round his ankle. As she comes level he turns his head toward her without lifting it, and his mouth moves, too faintly for you to make out anything.

The Warm One lowers his jaw onto his forelegs again, facing the door, as though none of you had ever come in.

"*Not yet,*" he says to the yellow bead. "I have had *not yet* from that door every sleep for twenty winters. I can hear it once more from you." He doesn't raise his voice. "Go and find my piece. Take the sweeper. Read your name off it with your own eyes, since you would rather owe me nothing. But it is a piece of *my* door, and it comes back to this room in your hands. If I hear it go up my stair instead…" A slide of old coin runs off the bed and rings across the stone, without hurry. "…then I will not need sweepers anymore, and the Broom will learn what I do with things I do not need."

His breath goes out, long and even, and the ruin breathes with it.

"One sleep. Not two."

Sefa is back at the arch beside Bram.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 18

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't pick up the dagger. He lets it lie on the threshold stone beside his boot and speaks to Sefa without taking his eyes off the dragon's head.

"Take it up. We don't leave iron on his floor."

Then his voice goes out flat across the chamber, not loud, but meant to carry to every wall.

"One sleep. We'll use it." He steps back to the arch and turns just enough to let Sefa come through first, then gestures Pell over with a jerk of his head. "We search the burned room, then the path back to the stair. Ixx comes. And if any of the Broom come with us, they come because it's their house and they choose to—not because I carried them."

He keeps the shield facing the room until Pell is with him, and only then turns toward the dark of the hot hall.

"The piece comes back here in our hands, to this room, before the day's out. So you'll have no cause to find out what you do with things you don't need. I'll not hand you that excuse tonight."

Then he's moving, first back out into the heat, and the lamp on the shield rim cuts the swept path out of the dark. To Ixx, low: "On your feet, sweeper. Show us the clean stones going back. All of us step where you step." A beat. "And tell me how many times you've swept this path for that plate—crack by crack. I'd like to know what I'm looking at before I go groping into a seam that breathes fire."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa stoops and picks up her dagger from the threshold stone, and sheathes it without looking at the lane. Her hand is not quite steady on the buckle; she lets it finish the job before she speaks.

"One sleep." She says it back to the dragon's turned head, level. "You'll have the piece in this room, in my hands, and you'll have my answer with it. I don't make a promise I can't keep, so I'm not promising you *yes*. I'm promising you *back*." A beat. "And you were right about the sweeping. I was wrong, and I said it in your house, and Ixx has corrected me. I'll not do it again."

Then she turns to the arch and the hall beyond it, and doesn't look at the hoard.

"Pell, you've got it. The burned room — the Broom won't go in, the four did, ten days ago with lamps, and a frightened man running from a light that bit him doesn't keep a piece of hot bronze in his hand all the way across *that* floor. Tracks go in; we follow them. Bram, same order back across, Ixx's broom and your boot, nobody hurries even though we'd like to." She crouches by the kobold still flat behind Bram's boot and keeps her voice for him. "Ixx. Up. You swept every crack for that piece and found nothing, and I was unfair to you about it. Walk us back across, and then stay in the dome with Hrask while we go into the paper room — you've done enough carrying today."

She straightens, and only then lets herself say the next thing, quietly, side-on to Bram and Pell, knowing the stone in this room is not deaf and choosing her words for it: "What the flame said, I'll give you word for word once we're past the string, where only the stair hears. It's short. It changes nothing about tonight: we find the piece first. Everything else waits on what's written on it."

She steps up to the arch, lays two fingers on the shield-rim to check the Light is still full, and gathers her hem out of the heat. "Lead on, Ixx. Here, then the broom says next."

Pell Dunmore: I move back to the arch with the others, leaving the scattered gold untouched.

“Ixx,” I say quietly, “we need your broom and your eyes for the crossing. Same way back, one stone at a time.”

I wait for him to rise, then take my place behind Bram. Once we’re moving away from the chamber, I speak low to my companions.

“The four went into that room *before* cutting the plate. Those tracks may not tell us where it ended up—but they could tell us what they knew. Let’s hear why the Broom won’t enter before we do.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa stoops for her dagger and buckles it on. Her fingers fumble the strap once before they get it. Bram's lines go out across the chamber, flat and even, and the Warm One doesn't lift his jaw from his forelegs. His eye stays on the yellow bead in the door.

"Then do not hand it to me," he says when Bram is done. He says nothing at all to Sefa's admission. His breath stops for a moment while she speaks and comes out again at its old pace, no warmer. From him, that may be the answer.

Ixx gets up off the floor. He picks up his broom and checks the bristles with one claw, then looks up at Sefa crouched beside him. He seems to have expected a kick more than an apology. "Sefa was not unfair," he says, very low. "Sefa did not know." He takes his place behind Bram's left elbow.

At the arch Bram keeps his shield toward the room until Pell has come over. Only then does he turn to the hall. The lamp-yellow light on the rim spreads across the first pale stones, and the red of the cracks lies beyond it.

"Here," Ixx says, and the broom touches down.

They go back the way they came, one stone at a time. Ixx answers Bram's question between stones, in pieces, and never says the next stone late.

"How many sweeps." *Here.* "Every sleep, from the sleep the four died. Ten." *Here.* "All Broom. Big ones, small ones. Every crack on the pale side, with claws, with sticks." *Wait.* A seam to the right goes orange and sighs, and sparks drift past the shield rim. "Now. Here, here." On the far side he goes on. "The bright side, not. Bright side, little fires walk. Broom sweeps up to the bright, not in." He thinks about it. "If the piece went in a bright crack, it is gone. Bronze goes soft in the bright. Like the cap."

Sefa had asked that question in the warren and got no answer. She has one now, and it's not a hopeful one.

They come level with the long black smear again. On the right, out past the chisel in its white-hot crack, a crack brightens and a little fire stands up out of it. It's the same knee-high hunch of embers, and it's further off this time, almost at the right-hand wall. This time it doesn't pace them. It stands there and its seams flare, the way a coal flares when it's blown on. For a breath it sheds a light of its own, a red-orange ring on the stones and the wall behind it.

The light lands on the wall, and Bram sees what the wall has there, because it's lit and he's looking. Between the last two pillars on that side there's a doorway in the dressed stone. It's narrow and square-topped, a man's height, and dark inside. Its sill sits across the brightest run of cracks in the hall. He saw nothing there on the way in, because the hall was dark past the shield-light and he was watching his feet.

Then the little fire folds down into its crack and the doorway goes back into the dark.

Ixx has seen where Bram's head turned. "Old door," he says, before Bram can ask. "Tall ones' door. Floor bites there, all the way." His tail goes round his ankle. "Broom does not go."

The rest of the crossing is broom, boot, boot, boot. Then the passage and its long climb, with the bronze channels whispering at their hips. The heat thins step by step until the dome's air feels almost cool, though it isn't. Hrask is still at the hides, spear upright, exactly where they left him. He sees four come back up out of the red and lets out a breath through his nose that might be relief.

Ixx stops at the edge of the grey dust fan in front of the burned arch. His toes are right at the line where the kobold prints stop. The boot prints go on past him, softened by ten days of draught, in under the blackened hinges.

Pell asked him on the way why the Broom won't go in, and he answers it now, looking into the dark of the arch and not at any of them.

"No ghosts," he says first, as if he's been asked that before. "Old ones say no ghosts. Is only old." He points his snout at the dark. "Tall shelves. Tall as Bram, taller. Paper on them, black, burned, soft like ash. Shelves lean. When Tisk was small, Tisk's egg-brother went in for paper to start a fire." He stops. "A shelf came down. All the paper, all the wood, on him. Broom dug. Long time." The tail tightens. "After, Tisk said: the paper room is the tall ones' room. Tall ones' things fall on small ones. Broom does not go."

He thinks, and adds the part he plainly finds strangest.

"The four went in. String sang. Middle of the sleep. Broom woke and came out, and the four were in the paper room already, with lamps. Broom waited here, at the dust. Long. Lamps went far in. Then gone." He lifts his eyes to Bram. "Broom did not see the four come out. Broom waited at the dust, and the four did not come out. Then the hot rooms shouted, and the four were in the hot rooms, on the floor." He spreads his small claws. "Ixx does not know the way they walked."

He steps back from the dust fan, one step and then another, until he's beside Hrask at the hides. Sefa told him to stay, and he does. He looks at the boot prints going in and not out, then at the three of them.

"Ixx waits here. Ixx sweeps the dust after, if the tall ones come out."

Under the arch the dark smells of old burned paper and nothing else. The shield-light reaches a stride in and finds the edge of a shelf: tall, black, leaning a little to the left.

*(Out of game: no dice this turn. Sefa's Light on the shield rim has about a quarter of an hour left. Pell's hooded lantern is filled and ready.)*

 *(Out of game: neither needs a die. Bram's orders, his words to the Warm One, holding his shield toward the room and leading the way back across are open speech and movement with nothing uncertain in them, and the turn plays all of it as he declared. Sefa picking up and sheathing her dagger, her words to the dragon and to Ixx, and checking the Light are true speech and plain actions, and true speech is never rolled. All of it happened as written.)*
````

````text
You asked that question in the warren and got no answer. You have one now, and it's not a hopeful one.

You come level with the long black smear again. On the right, out past the chisel in its white-hot crack, a crack brightens and a little fire stands up out of it. It's the same knee-high hunch of embers, further off this time, almost at the right-hand wall. This time it doesn't pace you. It stands there and its seams flare, the way a coal flares when it's blown on. For a breath it sheds a light of its own, a red-orange ring on the stones and the wall behind it. Bram's head turns toward it. Then the little fire folds down into its crack, and that side of the hall goes dark again.

Ixx has seen where Bram's head turned. "Old door," he says, before Bram can ask. "Tall ones' door. Floor bites there, all the way." His tail goes round his ankle. "Broom does not go."

The rest of the crossing is broom, boot, boot, boot. Then comes the passage and its long climb, with the bronze channels whispering at your hips. The heat thins step by step until the dome's air feels almost cool, though it isn't. Hrask is still at the hides, spear upright, exactly where you left him. He sees four come back up out of the red and lets out a breath through his nose that might be relief.

Ixx stops at the edge of the grey dust fan in front of the burned arch. His toes are right at the line where the kobold prints stop. The boot prints go on past him, softened by ten days of draught, in under the blackened hinges.

Pell asked him on the way why the Broom won't go in, and he answers it now, looking into the dark of the arch and not at any of you.

"No ghosts," he says first, as if he's been asked that before. "Old ones say no ghosts. Is only old." He points his snout at the dark. "Tall shelves. Tall as Bram, taller. Paper on them, black, burned, soft like ash. Shelves lean. When Tisk was small, Tisk's egg-brother went in for paper to start a fire." He stops. "A shelf came down. All the paper, all the wood, on him. Broom dug. Long time." The tail tightens. "After, Tisk said: the paper room is the tall ones' room. Tall ones' things fall on small ones. Broom does not go."

He thinks, and adds the part he plainly finds strangest.

"The four went in. String sang. Middle of the sleep. Broom woke and came out, and the four were in the paper room already, with lamps. Broom waited here, at the dust. Long. Lamps went far in. Then gone." He lifts his eyes to Bram. "Broom did not see the four come out. Broom waited at the dust, and the four did not come out. Then the hot rooms shouted, and the four were in the hot rooms, on the floor." He spreads his small claws. "Ixx does not know the way they walked."

He steps back from the dust fan, one step and then another, until he's beside Hrask at the hides. You told him to stay, and he does. He looks at the boot prints going in and not out, then at the three of you.

"Ixx waits here. Ixx sweeps the dust after, if the tall ones come out."

Under the arch the dark smells of old burned paper and nothing else. The shield-light reaches a stride in and finds the edge of a shelf: tall, black, leaning a little to the left.

*(Out of game: no dice this turn. Picking up and sheathing your dagger, your words to the dragon and to Ixx, and checking the Light were true speech and plain actions, and true speech is never rolled. All of it happened as you wrote. Your Light on the shield rim has about a quarter of an hour left. Pell's hooded lantern is filled and ready.)*
````

````text
Bram doesn't pick up the dagger. He lets it lie on the threshold stone beside his boot and speaks to Sefa without taking his eyes off the dragon's head: "Take it up. We don't leave iron on his floor."

Then his voice goes out flat across the chamber, not loud, but meant to carry to every wall: "One sleep. We'll use it." He steps back to the arch and turns just enough to let Sefa come through first, then jerks his head to call you over. "We search the burned room, then the path back to the stair. Ixx comes. And if any of the Broom come with us, they come because it's their house and they choose to — not because I carried them."

Sefa stoops for her dagger and buckles it on. Her fingers fumble the strap once before they get it. Then she speaks to the dragon's turned head, level:

"One sleep. You'll have the piece in this room, in my hands, and you'll have my answer with it. I don't make a promise I can't keep, so I'm not promising you *yes*. I'm promising you *back*." A beat. "And you were right about the sweeping. I was wrong, and I said it in your house, and Ixx has corrected me. I'll not do it again."

She turns to the arch and doesn't look at the hoard. "Pell, you've got it. The burned room — the Broom won't go in, the four did, ten days ago with lamps, and a frightened man running from a light that bit him doesn't keep a piece of hot bronze in his hand all the way across *that* floor. Tracks go in; we follow them. Bram, same order back across, Ixx's broom and your boot, nobody hurries even though we'd like to."

She crouches by the kobold flat behind Bram's boot and keeps her voice for him: "Ixx. Up. You swept every crack for that piece and found nothing, and I was unfair to you about it. Walk us back across, and then stay in the dome with Hrask while we go into the paper room — you've done enough carrying today."

Bram keeps his shield facing the room until you're with him, and only then turns toward the dark of the hot hall. "The piece comes back here in our hands, to this room, before the day's out. So you'll have no cause to find out what you do with things you don't need. I'll not hand you that excuse tonight."

Sefa straightens and speaks quietly, side-on to you and Bram: "What the flame said, I'll give you word for word once we're past the string, where only the stair hears. It's short. It changes nothing about tonight: we find the piece first. Everything else waits on what's written on it." She steps up to the arch, lays two fingers on Bram's shield-rim to check the Light, and gathers her hem out of the heat. "Lead on, Ixx. Here, then the broom says next."

The Warm One doesn't lift his jaw from his forelegs. His eye stays on the yellow bead in the door.

"Then do not hand it to me," he says when Bram is done. He says nothing at all to Sefa's admission. His breath stops for a moment while she speaks, then comes out again at its old pace, no warmer. From him, that may be the answer.

Ixx gets up off the floor. He picks up his broom and checks the bristles with one claw, then looks up at Sefa crouched beside him. He seems to have expected a kick more than an apology. "Sefa was not unfair," he says, very low. "Sefa did not know." He takes his place behind Bram's left elbow.

At the arch the lamp-yellow light on Bram's shield rim spreads across the first pale stones, and the red of the cracks lies beyond it.

"Here," Ixx says, and the broom touches down.

You go back the way you came, one stone at a time. Bram, in front, speaks low to Ixx: "On your feet, sweeper. Show us the clean stones going back. All of us step where you step." A beat. "And tell me how many times you've swept this path for that plate — crack by crack. I'd like to know what I'm looking at before I go groping into a seam that breathes fire."

Ixx answers him between stones, in pieces, and he never calls the next stone late.

"How many sweeps." *Here.* "Every sleep, from the sleep the four died. Ten." *Here.* "All Broom. Big ones, small ones. Every crack on the pale side, with claws, with sticks." *Wait.* A seam to the right goes orange and sighs, and sparks drift past the shield rim. "Now. Here, here." On the far side he goes on. "The bright side, not. Bright side, little fires walk. Broom sweeps up to the bright, not in." He thinks about it. "If the piece went in a bright crack, it is gone. Bronze goes soft in the bright. Like the cap."

You come level with the long black smear again. On the right, out past the chisel in its white-hot crack, a crack brightens and a little fire stands up out of it. It's the same knee-high hunch of embers, further off this time, almost at the right-hand wall. This time it doesn't pace you. It stands there and its seams flare, the way a coal flares when it's blown on. For a breath it sheds a light of its own, a red-orange ring on the stones and the wall behind it. Bram's head turns toward it. Then the little fire folds down into its crack, and that side of the hall goes dark again.

Ixx has seen where Bram's head turned. "Old door," he says, before Bram can ask. "Tall ones' door. Floor bites there, all the way." His tail goes round his ankle. "Broom does not go."

The rest of the crossing is broom, boot, boot, boot. Then comes the passage and its long climb, with the bronze channels whispering at your hips. Ixx stops at the edge of the grey dust fan in front of the burned arch. His toes are right at the line where the kobold prints stop. The boot prints go on past him, softened by ten days of draught, in under the blackened hinges.

He thinks, and adds the part he plainly finds strangest.

"The four went in. String sang. Middle of the sleep. Broom woke and came out, and the four were in the paper room already, with lamps. Broom waited here, at the dust. Long. Lamps went far in. Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]
````

## Round 19

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram stands at the edge of the dust and stares into the burned room, past his own shield-light. He turns what Ixx said over in his head, and the piece that won't sit still is this: the four went in there with lamps, the Broom waited at the dust, and then the four were in the hot rooms. Not through the dome. Through somewhere else.

He looks over his shoulder at Ixx, then at Pell.

"If they didn't come out here, then there's a way from that room into the hot rooms," he says. "That's how they got behind us, or would have. That's where we look." He shifts his grip on the shield and hooks a thumb at the leaning shelf. "And that means we don't go leaning through there like men hunting deer. We find the way through with our eyes first, and we don't let the shelves block the way back."

He glances at Sefa. "Light close. Pell, watch the floor for scrape-lines and for anything shiny—bronze, iron, broken shelf. I'll lead and watch the beams. If I stop, we all stop."

Then he takes a step into the old library, careful as he did on the stairs, and lets the lamp throw its yellow across the shelves and the dust. The dry smell of burned paper rises around them like old smoke woken by their passing.

"Talk low," he says. "Sound carries in a room this old. If there's a way through, I want to find it before it finds us."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa stands at the edge of the dust fan a moment, looking at the boot prints going in and not coming out, and lets Ixx's account settle into place.

"Then they didn't walk across the hot rooms at all," she says, low, to Bram and Pell. "They went *in here*, far in, and came out on the bright side — at that old door Ixx won't look at, where the little fire stood up and lit itself for us. That's how they reached the rim without the Broom seeing them cross. They cut the plate, the light bit, they ran *back* the way they'd come — toward the old door, not the stair — and the floor took them short of it." She looks at the dark under the hinges. "So the piece is either in this room, on the far side, or in the bright cracks between the smear and that door. The Broom swept the pale side ten times and found nothing because it was never on the pale side."

She turns to Ixx and Hrask. "Thank you. Stay by the small ones. If we're not out by the time the fire's burned down a cake, don't come in after us — send Skra up the stair as far as the bend and leave her there." A nod to Pell. "Light your lantern; mine has a quarter hour and I'd rather not put the Lantern's colour on every shelf in a dead wizard's library before I know what's written in here. Bram, shield up and watch what leans — Tisk's egg-brother died under one of these, and you're the tallest thing in the room. Pell, the prints. I'll read."

Then she goes in under the burned hinges, slowly, one hand out to the leaning shelf-edge but not pushing it, following in Pell's footsteps through the ash. As they go she reads — spines, labels, whatever is scratched on the shelf-ends or burned into the lintels of the inner doors — looking for anything that says *Cellar*, *lent*, *lantern*, *fire*, or Ulcaster's stepped tower. *(Eyes of the Rune Keeper on anything legible; Arcana if a title means something.)*

Once they are far enough among the stacks that the arch is a grey square behind them and no red breathes near, she stops, and says it quietly, word for word, as promised:

"When it flared, the voice said: *That light is mine. Take it back.*" A breath. "Not *open the door*. Not *free me*. *Take it back*. That's what I'm here for, whether I like the timing or not — and nothing in those words says the fire comes with it." Her hand rests on the Book, still warm. "One more thing. Mage Hand reaches thirty feet. If the piece is lying in a bright crack, nobody's boot goes near it — I'll lift it out from the pale side with a hand that can't burn. Now let's find where these four came out."

Pell Dunmore: “Another way through,” I say quietly. “Perhaps to that old door across the bright floor. Let’s see what the tracks tell us.”

To Ixx: “Leave the dust for now, please. It may save us some searching.”

I light my hooded lantern and hold it low at the threshold. Before entering, I study the nearest shelf’s lean, its supports, and the floor beneath it. I look for a route along the boot prints that won’t require brushing against shelves or stepping on fallen wood.

“Let me check the footing first. No pulling books, no leaning on anything.” I advance only as far as the light shows safe space, watching for disturbed dust, a dropped bronze plate, and anything rigged across the aisle.
````

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Arcana: what "the lent flame" means for the reading room of a wizards' school · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Arcana: what "the lent flame" means for the reading room of a wizards' school · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Ixx looks down at the grey fan of dust and then at Pell. He lifts his broom off the floor and holds it upright against his chest, well away from the dust.

"Ixx leaves it," he says. To Sefa he gives a single click and then remembers to say it in words. "Stays by small ones. Skra to the bend." Hrask says nothing. He has heard the shape of it, and his eyes go to the stack of dung cakes by the fire as if he's already counting them.

Pell opens the shutter of his hooded lantern and holds it low at the arch, and the dark under the hinges turns brown and grey. The first shelf is as tall as Bram and half again. Its uprights are oak, charred black on the side that faced the fire, and they're set into iron shoes on the floor that have rusted to scale. It leans left by about a hand's width at the top, and the lean is held by a crossbar wedged against its neighbour. That's an old repair or a lucky fall, and either way it has held a long time. The shelves are full of what used to be books. They're black blocks now, fused to each other along the spines, and the edges have gone soft and grey like the ash at the end of a cigar. The draught from the dome stirs them, and a little drifts down. The boot prints run straight between the first two shelves, down the middle of the aisle. The four men walked where the floor was clear and touched nothing, and Pell can read that in the dust too: the ash on the shelf-edges along the aisle hasn't been disturbed.

Bram goes in first. His shield-light runs up the shelves and loses itself in the dark of the ceiling. Pell is at his shoulder with the lantern, and Sefa comes last, setting her feet in Pell's prints.

The library is bigger than the dome. The aisle runs straight back from the arch between rows of shelves, maybe ninety feet, with cross-aisles every twenty. Near the end of the second row the light on Bram's shield thins, wavers and goes out, a few minutes short of a quarter hour, as Sefa said it would. For a moment there's only Pell's lantern, and the shelves seem to step closer.

Thirty feet in, a shelf has come down. It fell long ago, across the aisle and against the shelf opposite, and it lies there like a roof beam, so the aisle runs under it in a low triangle of dark. Under it the floor has been dug. Old claw-scrapes run through the ash, and books are pulled out and heaped to one side, gone so soft they've slumped into a single grey drift. Bram doesn't need anyone to tell him what that is. The boot prints go straight under the beam, bent low; the heels dig deeper there. Bram watches the beam as he ducks under it, the way he said he would. Where it rests on the other shelf the charred upright has split along its grain, and a line of pale wood shows in the split. It isn't moving. Fine ash sifts down onto his shield as the mail on his back passes under, and settles. Nobody breathes on it.

Sefa reads as they go. Most of what was written here is gone, burned into the black blocks or flaked off the shelf-ends with the char. Still, there are scraps. A shelf-end on the third row has a strip of bronze tacked to it, and the strip has kept its raised letters: *ON THE CARRYING OF HEAT*. Further back there's another: *WARDS — OF DOORS AND THRESHOLDS*. Under that, burned into the wood of the shelf, someone long ago scratched a tally of days, the way a bored apprentice does, with *nine more* beside it. Pell's lantern finds one book that the fire missed. It's lying flat on a lower shelf, as if somebody pulled it out and laid it down. Its spine reads *Accounts of the Lower Cellar, Year of* and the rest is gone. It's closed. Nobody touches it.

Among the stacks, with the arch a dim grey square behind them and no red anywhere near, Sefa stops and says what she promised to say, word for word, low. The library takes it in. The air is dead here, and it doesn't carry.

At the far end the aisle comes up against a wall of dressed stone, and the boot prints go to a door in it. It's stone too, a slab on bronze pins, and it stands open about a forearm's width. Nobody has burned it, and there's no char on it at all. Above it a short line is cut into the lintel, under a small version of Ulcaster's stepped tower. The cutting is crisp, protected all these centuries by the shelves that stood in front of it.

*The Apprentices' Room. No flame here but the lent one.*

A library. Paper. A room where wizards would rather nobody carried a candle. Sefa reads it twice and tries to make it mean more than a rule for students.

She can't get any further than the plain words. Every school has rules about flame in its libraries. A wizard could light a room a dozen ways without a wick, and nothing she has read tells her whether *lent* here means a power was bargained with or only a lamp borrowed from the master's study. She knows two words, *lent* and *flame*, and she knows where else she has seen them together. She doesn't know if that's a reason or a coincidence.

Pell sets the lantern to the gap and looks before anyone goes through. Then he reads the floor, which is his work and Bram's, and the floor is plain.

The room is small, perhaps twenty feet square, with a low vault and reading desks of stone built into the walls. The fire never got in here. There are shelves, with real books on them, warped and foxed and leather gone hard, but books. Someone has camped on the floor. There are two bedrolls, unrolled and left, a tin lamp on its side with its oil long burned off, and a heel of black bread gone stone-hard. A leather pack lies against the leg of the nearest desk, its flap open, and the corner of something folded in oilskin shows out of it. Beside it, flat on the stone, is a pair of heavy leather gloves. One is scorched brown across the palm.

On the far side of the room a second opening, square-topped and narrow like the old door Bram saw across the hall, gives onto steps going down. Warm air comes up them, and the struck-flint smell with it. The prints tell the story the room won't. Four sets of boots come in from the library, walk around the camp and go down those steps together. One set comes back up. It's long-strided and scuffed, with the toes digging in, a man taking steps two at a time. It crosses straight to the desk where the pack lies and stops there. The heel marks turn on the spot. Then the same boots go back down the steps the way they came, running again.

Pell's lantern is turned toward the steps. Behind it, by the desk leg, between the pack and the scorched glove, there's a light where there shouldn't be one.

It's very faint, no more than a coal at the bottom of a grate. It's yellow, lamp-yellow, and it isn't the lantern's.

At Sefa's belt the Book of Shadows is warm again.

 Sefa's Arcana fails. She reads the lintel twice and comes away with nothing more than the words themselves: *lent* and *flame*, and the place she has seen them together before.

Bram reads the beams as they go, and nothing in the library is set against them. He finds no cord, no pin and no loose stone, only age and fire damage. The one danger he marks is the fallen shelf thirty feet in, where its support has split along the grain, and they pass under it without touching it. At the reading room door he turns and looks back down the aisle with Pell's lantern behind him. The way out is the way they came: straight, open, and blocked by nothing except that low triangle under the fallen shelf, which a man in mail can stoop through without brushing it. Nothing has shifted behind them. Coming back, the arch is ninety feet off and they can walk it in a line.

*(Out of game: no rolls were needed for these. Spotting traps and checking the way back is Bram's road trade, nothing in the library was rigged, and Eyes of the Rune Keeper reads every inscription without a check. Only the meaning behind "the lent one" was uncertain, and that was the Arcana roll that failed.)*
````

Rolls: Sefa Marrin Arcana: what "the lent flame" means for the reading room of a wizards' school 12 vs 15 failure

````text
The library takes it in. The air is dead here, and it doesn't carry.

At the far end the aisle comes up against a wall of dressed stone, and the boot prints go to a door in it. It's stone too, a slab on bronze pins, and it stands open about a forearm's width. Nobody has burned it, and there's no char on it at all. Above it a short line is cut into the lintel, under a small version of Ulcaster's stepped tower. The cutting is crisp, protected all these centuries by the shelves that stood in front of it. Sefa reads it twice.

You read the beams as you go, and nothing in the library is set against them. You find no cord, no pin and no loose stone, only age and fire damage. The one danger you mark is the fallen shelf thirty feet in, where its support has split along the grain, and you passed under it without touching it. At the reading room door you turn and look back down the aisle with Pell's lantern behind you. The way out is the way you came: straight, open, and blocked by nothing except that low triangle under the fallen shelf, which a man in mail can stoop through without brushing it. Nothing has shifted behind you. Coming back, the arch is ninety feet off and you can walk it in a line.

Pell sets the lantern to the gap and looks before anyone goes through. Then you and he read the floor, and the floor is plain.

The room is small, perhaps twenty feet square, with a low vault and reading desks of stone built into the walls. The fire never got in here. There are shelves, with real books on them, warped and foxed and leather gone hard, but books. Someone has camped on the floor. There are two bedrolls, unrolled and left, a tin lamp on its side with its oil long burned off, and a heel of black bread gone stone-hard. A leather pack lies against the leg of the nearest desk, its flap open, and the corner of something folded in oilskin shows out of it. Beside it, flat on the stone, is a pair of heavy leather gloves. One is scorched brown across the palm.

On the far side of the room a second opening, square-topped and narrow like the old door you saw across the hall, gives onto steps going down. Warm air comes up them, and the struck-flint smell with it. The prints tell the story the room won't. Four sets of boots come in from the library, walk around the camp and go down those steps together. One set comes back up. It's long-strided and scuffed, with the toes digging in, a man taking steps two at a time. It crosses straight to the desk where the pack lies and stops there. The heel marks turn on the spot. Then the same boots go back down the steps the way they came, running again.

Pell's lantern is turned toward the steps. Behind it, by the desk leg, between the pack and the scorched glove, there's a light where there shouldn't be one.

It's very faint, no more than a coal at the bottom of a grate. It's yellow, lamp-yellow, and it isn't the lantern's.
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
She can't get any further than the plain words. Every school has rules about flame in its libraries. A wizard could light a room a dozen ways without a wick, and nothing you have read tells you whether *lent* here means a power was bargained with or only a lamp borrowed from the master's study. You know two words, *lent* and *flame*, and you know where else you have seen them together. You don't know if that's a reason or a coincidence.

At the reading room door Bram turns and looks back down the aisle with Pell's lantern behind him. Behind you the way out is the way you came: straight, open, ninety feet to the arch, with only that low triangle under the fallen shelf.

Pell sets the lantern to the gap and looks before anyone goes through. Then he and Bram read the floor, and the floor is plain.

The room is small, perhaps twenty feet square, with a low vault and reading desks of stone built into the walls. The fire never got in here. There are shelves, with real books on them, warped and foxed and leather gone hard, but books. Someone has camped on the floor. One is scorched brown across the palm.

The prints tell the story the room won't. Four sets of boots come in from the library, walk around the camp and go down those steps together. One set comes back up. It's long-strided and scuffed, with the toes digging in, a man taking steps two at a time. It crosses straight to the desk where the pack lies and stops there. The heel marks turn on the spot. Then the same boots go back down the steps the way they came, running again.

Pell's lantern is turned toward the steps. Behind it, by the desk leg, between the pack and the scorched glove, there's a light where there shouldn't be one.

At your belt the Book of Shadows is warm again.
````
