**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram stands at the edge of the dust and stares into the burned room, past his own shield-light. He looks over his shoulder at Ixx, then at you.

"If they didn't come out here, then there's a way from that room into the hot rooms," he says. "That's how they got behind us, or would have. That's where we look." He shifts his grip on the shield and hooks a thumb at the leaning shelf. "And that means we don't go leaning through there like men hunting deer. We find the way through with our eyes first, and we don't let the shelves block the way back."

He glances at Sefa. "Light close. Pell, watch the floor for scrape-lines and for anything shiny—bronze, iron, broken shelf. I'll lead and watch the beams. If I stop, we all stop."

Sefa stands at the edge of the dust fan a moment, looking at the boot prints going in and not coming out.

"Then they didn't walk across the hot rooms at all," she says, low, to Bram and you. "They went *in here*, far in, and came out on the bright side — at that old door Ixx won't look at, where the little fire stood up and lit itself for us. That's how they reached the rim without the Broom seeing them cross. They cut the plate, the light bit, they ran *back* the way they'd come — toward the old door, not the stair — and the floor took them short of it." She looks at the dark under the hinges. "So the piece is either in this room, on the far side, or in the bright cracks between the smear and that door. The Broom swept the pale side ten times and found nothing because it was never on the pale side."

She turns to Ixx and Hrask. "Thank you. Stay by the small ones. If we're not out by the time the fire's burned down a cake, don't come in after us — send Skra up the stair as far as the bend and leave her there." She nods to you. "Light your lantern; mine has a quarter hour and I'd rather not put the Lantern's colour on every shelf in a dead wizard's library before I know what's written in here. Bram, shield up and watch what leans — Tisk's egg-brother died under one of these, and you're the tallest thing in the room. Pell, the prints. I'll read."

Bram takes a step into the old library, careful as he was on the stairs, and lets the lamp throw its yellow across the shelves and the dust. The dry smell of burned paper rises around you like old smoke woken by your passing.

"Talk low," he says. "Sound carries in a room this old. If there's a way through, I want to find it before it finds us."

Ixx looks down at the grey fan of dust and then at you. He lifts his broom off the floor and holds it upright against his chest, well away from the dust.

"Ixx leaves it," he says. To Sefa he gives a single click and then remembers to say it in words. "Stays by small ones. Skra to the bend." Hrask says nothing. He has heard the shape of it, and his eyes go to the stack of dung cakes by the fire as if he's already counting them.

You open the shutter of your hooded lantern and hold it low at the arch, and the dark under the hinges turns brown and grey. The first shelf is as tall as Bram and half again. Its uprights are oak, charred black on the side that faced the fire, and they're set into iron shoes on the floor that have rusted to scale. It leans left by about a hand's width at the top, and the lean is held by a crossbar wedged against its neighbour. That's an old repair or a lucky fall, and either way it has held a long time. The shelves are full of what used to be books. They're black blocks now, fused to each other along the spines, and the edges have gone soft and grey like the ash at the end of a cigar. The draught from the dome stirs them, and a little drifts down. The boot prints run straight between the first two shelves, down the middle of the aisle. The four men walked where the floor was clear and touched nothing, and you can read that in the dust too: the ash on the shelf-edges along the aisle hasn't been disturbed.

Bram goes in first. His shield-light runs up the shelves and loses itself in the dark of the ceiling. You are at his shoulder with the lantern, and Sefa comes last, setting her feet in your prints.

The library is bigger than the dome. The aisle runs straight back from the arch between rows of shelves, maybe ninety feet, with cross-aisles every twenty. Near the end of the second row the light on Bram's shield thins, wavers and goes out, a few minutes short of a quarter hour, as Sefa said it would. For a moment there's only your lantern, and the shelves seem to step closer.

Thirty feet in, a shelf has come down. It fell long ago, across the aisle and against the shelf opposite, and it lies there like a roof beam, so the aisle runs under it in a low triangle of dark. Under it the floor has been dug. Old claw-scrapes run through the ash, and books are pulled out and heaped to one side, gone so soft they've slumped into a single grey drift. The boot prints go straight under the beam, bent low; the heels dig deeper there. Bram watches the beam as he ducks under it. Where it rests on the other shelf the charred upright has split along its grain, and a line of pale wood shows in the split. It isn't moving. Fine ash sifts down onto his shield as the mail on his back passes under, and settles. Nobody breathes on it.

Sefa reads as you go, her eyes on the shelf-ends. Most of what was written here is gone, burned into the black blocks or flaked off the shelf-ends with the char. Still, there are scraps. A shelf-end on the third row has a strip of bronze tacked to it, its raised letters still standing. Further back there's another. Under that, burned into the wood of the shelf, someone long ago scratched a tally of days, the way a bored apprentice does. Your lantern finds one book that the fire missed. It's lying flat on a lower shelf, as if somebody pulled it out and laid it down, lettering still on what's left of its spine. It's closed. Nobody touches it.

Among the stacks, with the arch a dim grey square behind you and no red anywhere near, Sefa stops and says it quietly, word for word:

"When it flared, the voice said: *That light is mine. Take it back.*" A breath. "Not *open the door*. Not *free me*. *Take it back*. That's what I'm here for, whether I like the timing or not — and nothing in those words says the fire comes with it." Her hand rests on the Book at her belt. "One more thing. Mage Hand reaches thirty feet. If the piece is lying in a bright crack, nobody's boot goes near it — I'll lift it out from the pale side with a hand that can't burn. Now let's find where these four came out."

The library takes it in. The air is dead here, and it doesn't carry.

At the far end the aisle comes up against a wall of dressed stone, and the boot prints go to a door in it. It's stone too, a slab on bronze pins, and it stands open about a forearm's width. Nobody has burned it, and there's no char on it at all. Above it a short line is cut into the lintel, under a small version of Ulcaster's stepped tower. The cutting is crisp, protected all these centuries by the shelves that stood in front of it. Sefa reads it twice.

At the door Bram turns and looks back down the aisle past your lantern.

You set the lantern to the gap and look before anyone goes through. Then you read the floor, which is your work and Bram's, and the floor is plain.

The room is small, perhaps twenty feet square, with a low vault and reading desks of stone built into the walls. The fire never got in here. There are shelves, with real books on them, warped and foxed and leather gone hard, but books. Someone has camped on the floor. There are two bedrolls, unrolled and left, a tin lamp on its side with its oil long burned off, and a heel of black bread gone stone-hard. A leather pack lies against the leg of the nearest desk, its flap open, and the corner of something folded in oilskin shows out of it. Beside it, flat on the stone, is a pair of heavy leather gloves. One is scorched brown across the palm.

On the far side of the room a second opening, square-topped and narrow like the old door across the hall, gives onto steps going down. Warm air comes up them, and the struck-flint smell with it. The prints tell the story the room won't. Four sets of boots come in from the library, walk around the camp and go down those steps together. One set comes back up. It's long-strided and scuffed, with the toes digging in, a man taking steps two at a time. It crosses straight to the desk where the pack lies and stops there. The heel marks turn on the spot. Then the same boots go back down the steps the way they came, running again.

Your lantern is turned toward the steps. Behind it, by the desk leg, between the pack and the scorched glove, there's a light where there shouldn't be one.

It's very faint, no more than a coal at the bottom of a grate. It's yellow, lamp-yellow, and it isn't your lantern's.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 20

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram goes still at the threshold, his eyes on that ember of yellow light near the pack. He doesn't put a boot into the room yet.

"Sefa," he says quietly. "That's not firelight. That's something else, and it's sat right where one running man stopped, at the desk. Pell—hold where you are. Nobody touches that pack or the glove until we know what that light's coming from."

His shield stays up, and he studies the room the way he'd scan a camp with a half-seen fire in the edge of the dark.

"The four came in from the library, camped here, went down that stair together. One came back up, running, took something from the pack or put something down, then ran back down." He points with his chin at the single boot-print pattern on the floor, then at the oilskin protruding from the pack. "That's where their own gear lies. Could be the plate ended up in a satchel or a cloth. Could be the one who ran back dropped it here and couldn't pick it up again."

He turns his head just enough to glance at Sefa. "You read this place. Tell me what that lintel said before we go further."

And, low, to Pell: "Check the floor just inside for trip-wire and for where the boots stop. Then I'll go in first, shield-side to the light, and we work the room by lantern. Stay close and don't step between Sefa and that yellow glow."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa stops in the doorway with one hand on the cold stone of the jamb and doesn't go further until she's said it.

"Pell. Lower the lantern a moment. By the desk leg — between the pack and the glove." Her voice is barely above the dead air. "That's not your light, and it isn't mine. It's the colour of the thing on his door."

She reads the floor the way Pell showed her, aloud, so they all have it. "Four went down those steps. One came back up — fast, two at a time — straight to this desk, stopped, turned on the spot, and went back *down*. Running. Not up to the stair, not out through the library. Down, toward the others." She looks at the scorched glove. "He had the piece in his hand and it was burning him through the leather. He came back here to put it down, or to put it in the pack with whatever's in the oilskin — and then he went back for his friends. That's the one Tisk's people found nearest the old door."

Then she goes in, slowly, around the edge of the camp the way she'd cross a ward past sleeping beds, and lowers herself to her knees an arm's length from the desk leg — no closer. Her dagger stays sheathed; her hands stay open on her thighs. The Book is warm at her hip and she doesn't look at it.

"Bram, the steps. If anything comes up them, I want to hear about it before it's in the room. Pell — the pack and the oilskin are yours, but not yet. Nobody's hand near this until I've looked."

She leans and looks. First at the light itself: what it sits in — bare stone, the glove's shadow, a sliver of bronze — whether it's the colour of the bead in the door or the colour of the flare, whether it brightens now she's kneeling in front of it the way the lantern leaned. Then at whatever it sits on, for letters. If there is bronze and there are letters, she reads them, twice, before she lets herself understand them. *(Eyes of the Rune Keeper.)*

Only after that, quietly: "I'm not touching it with skin. Not after what it did to that glove." She lifts one hand and a faint shimmer of nothing takes shape above the desk — the Mage Hand — and hangs there, waiting. "If it's loose, I'll lift it onto the oilskin and we read it in the open. If it's wedged, we think again."

To the warmth at her belt, in the ninth-night place, not aloud: *I see it. Is that you, or is it his?*

Pell Dunmore: “One came back,” I whisper, tracing his route with a finger without touching the dust. “Stopped at that pack, then ran downstairs again. And there’s yellow light beside the glove—not mine.”

I keep the lantern clear of the old paper and angle its shutter to shade that spot from its light.

“Don’t move the pack yet. Let me see what he left.”

From the doorway, then closer only if the footing is clear, I examine the glow and the space around the pack for the missing plate, wires, or anything connected to it. I touch nothing yet.

“Sefa, what does the lintel say?”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Nobody moves at first. Bram stays in the gap of the slab door with his shield up and his eyes on the yellow light, and says what he has to say quietly. Sefa lays her hand on the stone of the jamb. The jamb is cool, the first cool stone any of them has touched since the stair. Pell's lantern dips.

Pell tilts the shutter until the beam lies across the floor and stops short of the desk. As the lantern light pulls back, the other light gets clearer. It's a thin yellow line on the stone, low and steady, in the shape of something curved. It's coming out from under whatever lies there.

While Sefa reads the prints aloud, Pell traces the runner's route with one finger above the dust, then goes over the floor inside the gap. Reading a floor is his trade and it doesn't take long. The flags are whole and dry, with no cord, no pins in the mortar, no loose stone, and nothing tied to the desk, the pack or the glove. The only marks in the dust are the four men's, crossed and recrossed around their camp, and the one set of long, scuffed strides that comes up out of the side stair and stops dead at the desk. In front of the desk leg the heel marks turn hard on the spot and dig in. Pell finds no wire and no trap. He also finds no other piece of bronze anywhere in the room.

"Clear," he breathes. "Nothing rigged."

Sefa is already inside. She turned sideways for the gap and went in round the edge of the camp, past the bedrolls, and knelt an arm's length from the desk leg with her hands open on her thighs. Bram comes in after Pell gives the word. The opening is a forearm wide, and he has to turn side-on and go through shield first, with his mail scraping the stone on both sides. Once inside he sets himself between the side stair and the desk with his shield toward the yellow light, as he said he would. From there the lantern shows him all of the small room.

Then Sefa looks.

It's lying on bare stone in the gap between the pack and the scorched glove. It's a strip of pale bronze about a hand long and two fingers deep, bent in a shallow curve, the curve you'd get from cutting a short length out of a great ring. Both ends are cut clean and straight, and at each end a half of a rivet hole shows, sawn through the middle. This is a plate from the door, and Pell can see it from where he stands. It's the ninth one, the plate that wasn't on the rim.

The light is underneath it. It leaks out round the edges onto the stone the way lamplight leaks out under a door. As Sefa kneels in front of it, the line of light gets wider, by about the width of a fingernail, and stays that way. At her hip the Book of Shadows warms, the slow way a cup warms when you hold it in both hands.

The face of the plate turned up toward her is written on. The letters are the same old Chondathan hand as the band on the door, worked into the bronze and filled with a fine grey dust that the room's dead air never stirred. The pact's sight reads them as easily as it read the door. Sefa reads the line twice.

At the left-hand end, sliced in half by the cut, is the other half of the *y* in *by*. Then comes:

*the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch, a lamp for a lamp*

At the right-hand end, also cut in half, is the first stroke of the dash that begins the band's next words: *and it is owed.*

Put together with the band on the door, it reads: *The light is lent by the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch, a lamp for a lamp, and it is owed.*

The dead air of the room takes that in, and nothing in the room answers.

She asks her question in the ninth-night place and doesn't say it aloud. The Book stays warm. The light under the plate stays a fingernail wider than it was.

Above the desk, the air thickens into the vague shape of a hand. The Mage Hand slides its fingers under the curve of the bronze and lifts. The plate comes up easily. Nothing holds it to the floor, and the stone underneath is clean, with a curved mark in the dust where the plate has lain for ten days.

As the plate rises, it turns in the spectral fingers, and they all see the other side.

Cut small and fine at one end of the underside is Ulcaster's stepped tower, no bigger than a thumbnail. Beside it is a lantern, hooded and ringed, the same lantern as over the stair and on the door. A flame burns in its heart the size of a barleycorn, lamp-yellow and perfectly still, and now that nothing covers it, its light spreads out. It's a soft glow, too weak to read by, about the reach of two strides. It lies over the desk, the pack and the scorched glove, and touches the toe of Bram's boot. Under it the scorch on the glove's palm shows plainly: a long curved burn, a hand long and two fingers deep. A man held this in his fist and kept holding it while it burned him through the leather.

The Mage Hand settles the plate on the corner of the oilskin fold where it sticks out of the open pack, lantern side up. Then the hand thins and goes.

The bronze lies there in its own small light. The warm air comes up the side stair from below, and the struck-flint smell comes with it.

*(Out of game: no dice this round. Bram's and Pell's question about the lintel is waiting on Sefa's player, since she read it last round.)*
````

Pell whispers, tracing a route through the air with one finger without touching the dust: "One came back. Stopped at that pack, then ran downstairs again. Then you look.

It's lying on bare stone in the gap between the pack and the scorched glove. It's a strip of pale bronze about a hand long and two fingers deep, bent in a shallow curve, the curve you'd get from cutting a short length out of a great ring. Both ends are cut clean and straight, and at each end a half of a rivet hole shows, sawn through the middle. This is a plate from the door. It's the ninth one, the plate that wasn't on the rim.

The light is underneath it. It leaks out round the edges onto the stone the way lamplight leaks out under a door. As you kneel in front of it, the line of light gets wider, by about the width of a fingernail, and stays that way. At your hip the Book of Shadows warms, the slow way a cup warms when you hold it in both hands.

The face of the plate turned up toward you is written on. The letters are the same old Chondathan hand as the band on the door, worked into the bronze and filled with a fine grey dust that the room's dead air never stirred. The pact's sight reads them as easily as it read the door. You read the line twice.

At the left-hand end, sliced in half by the cut, is the other half of the *y* in *by*. Then comes:

*the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch, a lamp for a lamp*

At the right-hand end, also cut in half, is the first stroke of the dash that begins the band's next words: *and it is owed.*

Put together with the band on the door, it reads: *The light is lent by the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch, a lamp for a lamp, and it is owed.*

The dead air of the room takes that in, and nothing in the room answers.

You ask your question in the ninth-night place. The Book stays warm. The light under the plate stays a fingernail wider than it was.

Above the desk, the air thickens into the vague shape of a hand. The Mage Hand slides its fingers under the curve of the bronze and lifts. The plate comes up easily. Nothing holds it to the floor, and the stone underneath is clean, with a curved mark in the dust where the plate has lain for ten days.

As the plate rises, it turns in the spectral fingers, and you all see the other side.

Cut small and fine at one end of the underside is Ulcaster's stepped tower, no bigger than a thumbnail. Beside it is a lantern, hooded and ringed, the same lantern as over the stair and on the door. A flame burns in its heart the size of a barleycorn, lamp-yellow and perfectly still, and now that nothing covers it, its light spreads out. It's a soft glow, too weak to read by, about the reach of two strides. It lies over the desk, the pack and the scorched glove, and touches the toe of Bram's boot. Under it the scorch on the glove's palm shows plainly: a long curved burn, a hand long and two fingers deep. A man held this in his fist and kept holding it while it burned him through the leather.

The Mage Hand settles the plate on the corner of the oilskin fold where it sticks out of the open pack, lantern side up. Then the hand thins and goes.

The bronze lies there in its own small light. The warm air comes up the side stair from below, and the struck-flint smell comes with it.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram stays still at the threshold, his eyes on the yellow light near the pack. "Sefa," he says quietly. "That's not firelight. That's something else, and it's sat right where one running man stopped, at the desk. Pell—hold where you are. Nobody touches that pack or the glove until we know what that light's coming from."

His shield stays up. "The four came in from the library, camped here, went down that stair together. One came back up, running, took something from the pack or put something down, then ran back down." He points with his chin at the single line of boot-prints on the floor, then at the oilskin sticking out of the pack. "That's where their own gear lies. Could be the plate ended up in a satchel or a cloth. Could be the one who ran back dropped it here and couldn't pick it up again." He glances at Sefa. "You read this place. Tell me what that lintel said before we go further."

Then, low, to you: "Check the floor just inside for trip-wire and for where the boots stop. Then I'll go in first, shield-side to the light, and we work the room by lantern. Stay close and don't step between Sefa and that yellow glow."

Sefa stops in the doorway with one hand on the cold stone of the jamb. "Pell. Lower the lantern a moment. By the desk leg — between the pack and the glove." Her voice is barely above the dead air. "That's not your light, and it isn't mine. It's the colour of the thing on his door."

She reads the floor aloud: "Four went down those steps. One came back up — fast, two at a time — straight to this desk, stopped, turned on the spot, and went back *down*. Running. Not up to the stair, not out through the library. Down, toward the others." She looks at the scorched glove. "He had the piece in his hand and it was burning him through the leather. He came back here to put it down, or to put it in the pack with whatever's in the oilskin — and then he went back for his friends. That's the one Tisk's people found nearest the old door."

She goes in slowly and kneels an arm's length from the desk leg, her hands open on her thighs. "Bram, the steps. If anything comes up them, I want to hear about it before it's in the room. Pell — the pack and the oilskin are yours, but not yet. Nobody's hand near this until I've looked."

Nobody moves at first. Bram stays in the gap of the slab door with his shield up and his eyes on the yellow light. Sefa lays her hand on the stone of the jamb. Your lantern dips.

You tilt the shutter until the beam lies across the floor and stops short of the desk. As the lantern light pulls back, the other light gets clearer. It's a thin yellow line on the stone, low and steady, in the shape of something curved. It's coming out from under whatever lies there.

You go over the floor inside the gap. Reading a floor is your trade and it doesn't take long. The flags are whole and dry, with no cord, no pins in the mortar, no loose stone, and nothing tied to the desk, the pack or the glove. The only marks in the dust are the four men's, crossed and recrossed around their camp, and the one set of long, scuffed strides that comes up out of the side stair and stops dead at the desk. In front of the desk leg the heel marks turn hard on the spot and dig in. You find no wire and no trap. You also find no other piece of bronze anywhere in the room.

Sefa went in sideways through the gap and round the edge of the camp, past the bedrolls, to where she now kneels. Bram comes in after you give the word. The opening is a forearm wide, and he has to turn side-on and go through shield first, with his mail scraping the stone on both sides. Once inside he sets himself between the side stair and the desk with his shield toward the yellow light, as he said he would.

Then Sefa looks.

It's lying on bare stone in the gap between the pack and the scorched glove. It's a strip of pale bronze about a hand long and two fingers deep, bent in a shallow curve, the curve you'd get from cutting a short length out of a great ring. Both ends are cut clean and straight, and at each end a half of a rivet hole shows, sawn through the middle. This is a plate from the door, and you can see it from where you stand. It's the ninth one, the plate that wasn't on the rim.

The light is underneath it. It leaks out round the edges onto the stone the way lamplight leaks out under a door. As Sefa kneels in front of it, the line of light gets wider, by about the width of a fingernail, and stays that way. The face of the plate turned up toward her is written on, letters worked into the bronze and filled with a fine grey dust. Sefa studies them for a while.

The dead air of the room is still, and nothing in it answers.

Sefa lifts one hand. "I'm not touching it with skin. Not after what it did to that glove." Above the desk, the air thickens into the vague shape of a hand. "If it's loose, I'll lift it onto the oilskin and we read it in the open. If it's wedged, we think again."

The spectral hand slides its fingers under the curve of the bronze and lifts. The plate comes up easily. Nothing holds it to the floor, and the stone underneath is clean, with a curved mark in the dust where the plate has lain.

As the plate rises, it turns in the spectral fingers, and you all see the other side.

Cut small and fine at one end of the underside is Ulcaster's stepped tower, no bigger than a thumbnail. Beside it is a lantern, hooded and ringed, the same lantern as over the stair and on the door. A flame burns in its heart the size of a barleycorn, lamp-yellow and perfectly still, and now that nothing covers it, its light spreads out. It's a soft glow, too weak to read by, about the reach of two strides. It lies over the desk, the pack and the scorched glove, and touches the toe of Bram's boot. Under it the scorch on the glove's palm shows plainly: a long curved burn, a hand long and two fingers deep. A man held this in his fist and kept holding it while it burned him through the leather.

The hand settles the plate on the corner of the oilskin fold where it sticks out of the open pack, lantern side up. Then the hand thins and goes.

The bronze lies there in its own small light. The warm air comes up the side stair from below, and the struck-flint smell comes with it.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 21

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram keeps his place, shield angled toward the side stair, and lets his eyes move between Sefa and the dark beyond it.

"That's the piece," he says, low. "And it's lit underneath. The same lamp in the carved lantern. I'd say it's been lying there quietly like an ember, waiting. The man dropped it and ran because he couldn't hold it."

He doesn't kneel or move toward the desk. He can read the shape from where he stands: clean-cut bronze, rivet halves, Ulcaster's tower and the lantern cut into the underside, and the small still flame glowing in its heart.

"Sefa—read what's on it. All of it, aloud. If it brightens, put it down and step back. No skin to it." Then he jerks his chin toward the pack. "Pell, see what's in that oilskin. Use a strap or a hooked tool, not your fingers. If they had a cold-iron chisel or a cut section with a charge left in it, I don't want it touching your hand."

He shifts half a step, putting his weight between the desk and the side stair, and turns his head enough to listen down it. The warm air moves past him, slow and dry, carrying the same flint-and-stone smell as the hot rooms.

"That stair goes the wrong way. Likely out at that old door we saw from the hall. So we read here, we search here, and then we go back by the library—not down there. If anything moves on those steps, I'll call." His voice is level, but his hand rests near his hip.

Sefa Marrin: Sefa doesn't get up. She stays on her knees an arm's length from the desk, with the little barleycorn of light lying on the oilskin in front of her and her hands still open on her thighs, and for a moment she doesn't say anything at all. Then she says it, the way she promised — word for word, flat, so there's no chance of her colouring it.

"*The light is lent by the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch, a lamp for a lamp, and it is owed.*" A breath. "That's the whole band, with the gap filled. The lintel, the door, and this — it's one sentence, cut in three places."

Her thumb finds the beads at her throat and stays there.

"The ninth night." She says it to the plate, not to them. "I always thought that was *my* ninth night. The oil ran out on the ninth night and I thought that was why it came — because I'd waited that long. It wasn't. That's its *name*. It keeps the ninth watch. The last one, when every other lamp's gone out." She looks at the barleycorn flame, which hasn't flickered. "And the price was never gold. *A lamp for a lamp.* The house of Ulcaster borrowed a light to hold their fire down, and what they promised back was — a lamp. Another one. Lit, and carried." Her voice is very level. "I think I know now why it answered a novice in the dark and nobody else. It was looking for the second lamp. It's had six years to see whether I'd burn steady."

She lets that sit in the dead air a moment, then makes herself turn her head and look at the scorched glove, and at the long-strided prints going back down the steps.

"He couldn't hold it. It burned him through the leather and he still ran back for the others with his hand like that. Whoever he was, he was braver than I've been giving him credit for." Quieter: "And it *didn't* burn the floor it lay on for ten days, and it isn't burning the oilskin now. It burned a hand that cut it off a door. That's not heat. That's a *no*."

She stands, slowly, knees stiff from the stone, and only now looks at the other two.

"Here's where I am. The Warm One wants this back in his room in my hands by one sleep, and I said *back*, so back it goes — but the thing that's lit on it is the same thing that's lit in me, and it's asking to go *home*, not into a dragon's bed. I don't yet know if home means the door, or means me, or means somewhere I haven't seen." A tip of her head at the dark square of the side stair and the flint smell coming up it. "The four went down there together. Whatever's at the bottom, they thought it was worth more than the door — they went down *before* they ever cut the plate. And one of them came back up here and then went down *again*, running toward it. I want to know what they were running toward."

"But not blind, and not before dark catches us underground with two slots and a dragon counting cakes." She nods at the pack. "Pell — the oilskin's yours now. Mind the plate; lift the cloth from the far side and don't let your skin near the bronze. Whatever he folded up in there, he thought it worth coming back for with his hand on fire. Bram — those steps. Hear them for me while he works. I'll read whatever's in the pack, and then I'll tell you what I think *a lamp for a lamp* costs, because I think one of you is going to have to tell me if I'm wrong about it."

She touches the Book at her belt, still warm as a held cup, and in the ninth-night place says only: *I read your name. I didn't know you had one. Is the door home — or am I?*

*(Religion on "the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch" if the Faith knows that title — I'd like to know whether it's a name the temples would recognise, or something only the wizards called it.)*

Pell Dunmore: “That’s our ninth plate. Both cut rivet holes match.”

I watch where the bronze rests on the oilskin for smoke or scorching. “Sefa, keep that hand ready. If it’s still hot, bare stone’s safer than their papers.”

I set my lantern securely on a clear patch of stone. Once the plate is safely clear, I examine the open pack and carefully unfold the oilskin, preserving any writing and keeping everything away from the lantern flame.

“Let’s see what they came here knowing. And tell us what you’ve read—lintel and plate—before we carry it anywhere.”
````

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Religion: does the Faith know "the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch"? · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Religion: does the Faith know "the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch"? · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Nobody moves straight away. In the dead air of the reading room everyone can hear the warm draught breathing up the side stair, and the bronze on the oilskin glows with its own small light.

Bram puts his weight half a step over so that he stands between the desk and the dark square of the steps. He says his piece low and leaves his hand near his hip. Sefa stays on her knees and gives them the plate's words aloud and flat, the way she promised: *The light is lent by the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch, a lamp for a lamp, and it is owed.* After that comes a long silence. Then she says the rest, about the ninth night and the second lamp and the glove that was burned through. She says it to the plate as much as to them.

While she talks she reaches for the title. She has heard every liturgy the Gate's hospice sisters knew.

The title comes back to her whole, as if someone had set the book open in front of her.

No god in any temple she knows answers to *the One Who Keeps the Ninth Watch*. It isn't a god's name, and the shape of it tells her so. The old celestial texts on the hospice shelves name the servants of the upper planes by their office and never by their names: *the One Who Bears*, *the One Who Waits at the Ford*. These are keepers and messengers, things that answer to something greater. Whose servant this one is, the shape of the title doesn't say.

She does know the watch itself. The Gate's temples divide the night into eight watches, and the hospice kept the same hours, a sister to each. Some of the oldest Lathanderite morning hymns, the kind the Song of the Morning still sings in Beregost, count one more. The ninth watch is the one nobody is set to keep, after the last lamp gutters and before the sun. She can hear the line in an old sister's cracked voice on a dark ward: *When the eighth lamp is out, the Ninth keeps the door till morning.* It's poetry, a hymn about the door of dawn. Lathander's priests sing of a great many things at that door.

Then a smaller memory follows, from the hospice and not from any book. When a shrine lent a lamp to light a sickroom, the custom of the Lower City was that it went back with a second lamp lit from its flame. You didn't return the lamp alone. You returned it with one more light in the world than when you borrowed it. *A lamp for a lamp.* That's what the old sisters called it, and it was a courtesy, not a price.

She knows two readings of those words now, and they don't agree. As the second one settles, the heat at her hip eases a little, from a cup just filled to a hand held. The Book says nothing more than that.

Pell sets his hooded lantern down on a clear flag of bare stone, well away from the papers and the bedrolls, with the shutter turned so its light falls across the desk leg without striking the bronze. Then he crouches and looks before he does anything else. He doesn't need long. The oilskin under the plate is glossy and whole. It has no brown ring and no curl at the edge, and the dead air smells of nothing but old leather and older dust. Ten days on stone and a few minutes on cloth, and it has scorched nothing it lies on. The plate sits where the spectral hand set it, on the corner of the fold where it sticks out of the pack's mouth, lantern side up and lit.

Pell can see into the pack past it without touching anything. The oilskin fold runs down inside, flat, about the size of a letter folded in four. Beside it a leather purse sags against the bottom seam with its neck knotted tight with cord. It's heavy enough to pull the leather into the shape of what's inside it. There's also a coil of thin cord, a stoppered clay flask and a spare shirt rolled tight. It's a hired man's pack, packed by someone who expected to come back for it.

Bram listens to the steps the whole time. The warm air coming up them rises and falls in a long draw and a longer letting-go. It's faint here, worn thin by distance and turns of stone, but he knows that rhythm now. It holds its old pace. Far below, too far to judge, a seam sighs open and shuts. Once there's a small dry crackle, like green wood catching, and then nothing. Nothing comes up the steps. Nobody's claws and nobody's boots.

*(Out of game: the plate still sits on the corner of the oilskin, so Pell can't unfold it until someone moves the plate, whether by Sefa's Mage Hand or some way of Pell's own. Who does it, and how?)*

 *(Out of game: why none of these four got a roll.)*
1. Sefa reading the plate aloud is true speech, and her Eyes of the Rune Keeper reads any writing without a check. The one uncertain part, what the Faith knows of the title, was the Religion roll, and it came up a natural 20.
2. Bram's orders to Pell and Sefa are open speech with nothing uncertain in them, so they happened as he said them.
3. Pell checking for scorch and looking into the open pack without touching it is routine work in his trade, so it needs no card. Opening the oilskin waits on someone moving the plate off it, and I've left that for you to answer.
4. Bram listening down the stair is ordinary watchfulness, so it falls to his passive Perception. Nothing was coming up the steps for him to catch, only the draught and that far-off crackle.
````

I don't yet know if home means the door, or means me, or means somewhere I haven't seen." She tips her head at the dark square of the side stair. "The four went down there together. Whatever's at the bottom, they thought it was worth more than the door. They went down *before* they ever cut the plate. And one of them came back up here and then went down *again*, running toward it. I want to know what they were running toward."

"But not blind, and not before dark catches us underground with two slots and a dragon counting cakes." She nods at the pack. "Pell, the oilskin's yours now. Mind the plate. Lift the cloth from the far side and don't let your skin near the bronze. Whatever he folded up in there, he thought it worth coming back for with his hand on fire. Bram, those steps. Hear them for me while he works. I'll read whatever's in the pack, and then I'll tell you what I think *a lamp for a lamp* costs, because I think one of you is going to have to tell me if I'm wrong about it."

She touches the Book at her belt and goes quiet a moment.

For a moment nobody moves. In the dead air of the reading room you can all hear the warm draught breathing up the side stair, slow and dry, smelling of flint and stone. The bronze on the oilskin glows with its own small light. Bram has put his weight half a step over, so that he stands between the desk and the dark square of the steps, his hand resting near his hip, his head turned to listen down them.

You set your hooded lantern down on a clear flag of bare stone, well away from the papers and the bedrolls, with the shutter turned so its light falls across the desk leg without striking the bronze. Then you crouch and look before you do anything else. You don't need long. The oilskin under the plate is glossy and whole. It has no brown ring and no curl at the edge, and the dead air smells of nothing but old leather and older dust. Ten days on stone and a few minutes on cloth, and it has scorched nothing it lies on. The plate sits where the spectral hand set it, on the corner of the fold where it sticks out of the pack's mouth, lantern side up and lit.

You can see into the pack past it without touching anything. The oilskin fold runs down inside, flat, about the size of a letter folded in four. Beside it a leather purse sags against the bottom seam with its neck knotted tight with cord. It's heavy enough to pull the leather into the shape of what's inside it. There's also a coil of thin cord, a stoppered clay flask and a spare shirt rolled tight. It's a hired man's pack, packed by someone who expected to come back for it.

Nothing comes up the steps.

*(Out of game: the plate still sits on the corner of the oilskin, so you can't unfold it until someone moves the plate, whether by Sefa's Mage Hand or some way of your own. Who does it, and how?)*
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 22

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram stays by the side stair with his shield toward the opening, listening down into the dark while Pell works. His voice stays low, meant only for the room.

"One sentence cut in three places," he says. "The door says one part, the lintel another. The plate closes the deal. Sounds like Ulcaster's people never wanted it read whole by one set of eyes—or they wanted it read only by someone the light chose." He glances at Sefa, not long. "You, it seems."

His attention flicks back to the stair, then to the bronze resting on the oilskin. "I'll say this, Sefa. A lamp-for-a-lamp isn't the same as blood for debt. You carry it, but you didn't cut the plate or break the door. The light's been making sure of you. When it asks, it'll ask once, and you'll know the cost because it'll be the thing you least want to set down." He pauses. "Don't set it down before it asks, and don't pay for another man's cut."

He jerks his head toward the pack. "Find out what they came back for. If it's a chisel or a cold-iron wedge, we'll know they meant to work the rim all along—and if it's a book or a letter, it'll tell us who hired them." Then, without turning, he adds: "We leave by the library. Not down those steps. Not today."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa is quiet a moment after the title settles into place, and when she speaks she keeps her voice at the dead-air pitch of the room.

"It isn't a god. The Faith doesn't have a god by that name, and the shape of it says why — *the One Who Keeps*, *the One Who Bears*, *the One Who Waits at the Ford*. That's how the old celestial books name the servants of the upper planes: by their office, never their name. Something greater's messenger. Whose, the title doesn't say." Her thumb moves along the beads. "But I know the watch. The temples keep eight; the hospice kept eight, a sister to each. The oldest Lathanderite hymns count one more — the one nobody's set to keep, after the last lamp gutters and before the sun. *When the eighth lamp is out, the Ninth keeps the door till morning.* Poetry about the door of dawn." A breath. "So it's a door-keeper. Of course it is."

Then, more carefully, because this part changes things: "And *a lamp for a lamp* — I've been reading it like a wizard, as a price. It isn't. It's a Lower City custom. When a shrine lent you a lamp to light a sickroom, you sent it back with a second lamp lit from its flame. Not instead. *As well.* One more light in the world than when you borrowed. The old sisters called it a courtesy, not a debt." She looks at the barleycorn flame on the bronze. "The Book cooled a little when I thought that. From a full cup to a held hand. I don't know what that means yet, but I'm telling you because I said I would."

She lifts her hand, and the shimmer of the Mage Hand gathers again above the desk.

"I'll move it — nobody's skin. Pell, point me to a flag of bare stone where you want it, clear of the papers and your lantern." The spectral fingers slide under the curve of the plate and lift it off the oilskin, slow, and carry it a stride to the side, settling it lantern-up on bare floor where its small light can lie on nothing that burns. The hand thins and goes. "There. The oilskin's yours. Lift the fold from the far side, with a strap, as Bram said. If it's a chisel of cold iron, I want to see it before anyone's hand closes on it."

Her eyes go to Bram at the head of the steps. "Anything?"

And to the Book, in the ninth-night place, while Pell works: *A courtesy, then. Not a toll. Tell me I've read it right.*

Pell Dunmore: “Sefa, lift the plate with your Mage Hand onto the clear stone beside the desk, please. Then I can get the papers out without disturbing it.”

I wait until she’s moved it, then carefully withdraw and unfold the oilskin, keeping its contents together and clear of my lantern.

“And tell us your thought about the price. We’ll hear it—but ‘a lamp for a lamp’ isn’t enough for me to agree it means your life.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram says his piece toward the side stair, low, and keeps his shield on the opening. The draught coming up past his boots holds its old pace. It's a long draw and a longer letting-go, worn thin by distance. Nothing comes up the steps while he talks. Nothing comes while Sefa talks either. Once, far below, a seam sighs open and shuts, the way it has every few minutes since they came in.

Sefa says what she knows of the title and the watch and the old sisters' custom. Her voice stays at the pitch of the room, and the dead air takes it in. The Book at her hip stays as it was, warm like a held hand and no warmer. Whatever she asks it in the ninth-night place, it doesn't answer in words.

The Mage Hand gathers above the desk, a thickening in the air with fingers. It slides under the curve of the bronze and lifts it off the oilskin. The plate's small light swings as it moves and slides over the bedrolls, the dry lamp and the scorched glove. The hand carries it a stride to the left and lowers it onto a bare flag between the desk and the wall, lantern side up. Nothing near it can burn. The hand thins and is gone. The plate's glow settles into a soft yellow pool about two strides across, and the corner of the oilskin it was lying on is as glossy and unmarked as the rest.

Pell takes the fold by its far edge and draws it up out of the pack. It's light and stiff, sealed with nothing but its own folds, and when he opens it there are two sheets of good paper inside, both dry. Pell knows the difference. A hireling's letter comes on rag paper, and this is a counting-house's.

He lays them flat on the stone desk in the lantern's beam, well clear of the flame.

The first is a letter in a clerk's hand, neat and slanted, with no flourishes:

*To Harl Ostley and his three, by hand.*

*Open the lower cellar under the hill, as described to you. Bring me the book bound in iron, whole and unread. The beast below may be left to itself. Do not trouble with it.*

*Half in hand. Half at the Feldepost in Beregost, on delivery.*

*— O. Tarr, for Dellmere Iron Factors*

On the back, in a different hand that's broad and badly spelled, someone has jotted a list in charcoal:

*oil 3 fl. — rope — bred — candels, NOT to lite below — gloves HEVY x2, T. says it bites — brandy for Corrie's nerves*

The second sheet is a plan, drawn in ink by someone who knew how to draw. A stair runs down to a round room. A long hall is hatched across with fine lines and marked *heat*. A large circle at the end has a smaller ring drawn round it, cut into nine short arcs, and one arc low on the left is inked in solid. Beside the circle, in the clerk's slanting hand: *cold iron only. The light will not like it.* Off the round room a rectangle is marked *library*, with a smaller square at its far end. From the square a dotted line runs down and comes out on the far side of the hatched hall, labelled *side way — gallery.*

Nobody drew the beast.

The pack still sags at the bottom with the knotted purse, the coil of cord, the clay flask and the rolled shirt, all where they were. Pell's lantern lies across the two sheets. The plate's yellow light lies on the floor beside them, and the draught comes up the side stair, warm and slow and smelling of struck flint.
````

## Round 23

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram keeps his shield angled toward the stair and glances at the glowing bronze on the floor. "Once the papers are read, we'll know if they were grave-robbers or hired for a purpose. Names on paper are worth more than gold in a place like this," he says, voice low. "Read them quick, but don't rush. I'll keep listening."

He adjusts his stance slightly, feeling the heat of the stairway at his back, and lets his gaze sweep the room. The small camp, the bedrolls, the empty lamp, the dry bread—signs of men who had expected to be underground for a while. "If those papers name an employer, keep it close. The dragon doesn't get that name until we know what it's worth to him."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa reads the letter twice, the way she reads everything down here, and then once more aloud for the room, flat, charcoal list and all. When she gets to *brandy for Corrie's nerves* her voice doesn't change, but she stops a moment after it.

"Harl Ostley. Corrie. T., who said it bites." She looks at the scorched glove. "Tisk's people burned them proper and never knew their names. I'll give her these before we sleep."

Then she lays a finger beside two lines of the clerk's hand without touching the paper. "*Whole and unread.* And *the beast below may be left to itself.* Whoever O. Tarr is, he knew there was a dragon in this hill and sent four men past it for a book he didn't want them to open — and he knew about the plates and the cold iron before any of them had seen the door. That isn't a rumour from the Friendly Arm. Someone has read about this cellar in a room with a desk." She straightens. "Dellmere Iron Factors, Beregost. Half at the Feldepost on delivery. That's a name and a door, and I want it in my pack when we go up. Pell — fold them. The plan too; that dotted line is our proof the four came out at the old door, and it's drawn by someone who's been here or copied someone who was."

She turns to the bronze lying in its own small light on the bare flag.

"Now the plate. Bram, hear me out before you say no." Her hands are open at her sides. "It didn't burn the stone for ten days. It didn't burn the oilskin. It burned the man who sawed it off the door and *kept hold of it*. That's not heat; that's refusal. And it leaned for me." A breath. "I'm going to touch it with one fingertip for the length of one heartbeat. Not grip it. If it so much as warms, I take my hand away and we wrap it in the oilskin and carry it like that, and I'll owe you both a very long apology. But if it doesn't — then I know the *no* isn't for me, and I know that before I'm standing in front of the dragon deciding what to do with it. I'd rather learn it here, in a cool room, with Pell's satchel a stride away."

She kneels, lays the Book of Shadows on the stone beside her knee so its warmth is where she can see her hand and feel it at once, and reaches out slowly — one fingertip, to the plain bronze at the end farthest from the barleycorn flame, not the lantern itself. One heartbeat. Then she lifts her hand and looks at it.

Whatever it did or didn't do, she says next, getting up: "Then we go out by the library, under the beam one at a time, and back to the dome. I want to sit with Tisk and give her the names, and I want the three of us to decide tonight — not at his door tomorrow — what *back* is going to mean. He gets the plate in this room in my hands; I said so. What he gets besides, I haven't said." She picks up the Book and buckles it on, and it is warm as a held hand against her hip. "And Bram — thank you. *Don't pay for another man's cut.* I'll keep that."

Pell Dunmore: I read the letter and the notes aloud, then hold the plan where both can see it.

“O. Tarr, for Dellmere Iron Factors. They knew which plate to cut, what metal to use, and that it would hurt. Wanted the book whole and *unread*. This wasn’t four men stumbling onto treasure.”

I point to the charcoal list. “And candles specifically not to be lit below. Sefa, does that mean anything to you? Before we try lighting a second lamp.”

Once she’s examined the originals, I refold them in the oilskin. “I’d keep these as evidence. Someone at the Feldepost owes Harl and his three an accounting.”

I leave the purse and other belongings where they are. “We have the plate and their route. I’m for going back through the library together.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa reads the letter aloud twice in the dead air of the reading room, flat, charcoal list and all. Bram stays by the side stair and listens. At *brandy for Corrie's nerves* her voice doesn't change, but she stops for a moment before she goes on. Pell reads the list again under his breath after her, then holds the plan up in the lantern beam so both of them can see the nine short arcs, the one inked solid, and the dotted line that runs from this room down and out to the far side of the hatched hall.

Bram's eyes go round the camp while they read. Two bedrolls were unrolled and never rolled up again. The lamp was left to burn dry. The heel of bread was saved for later. These were men who planned to be down here a night or two and come back to their beds. The draught from the side stair goes on breathing at his back at its old pace.

Then Sefa kneels by the plate.

She sets the Book of Shadows on the stone beside her knee and reaches out slowly with one fingertip. She touches the plain end of the bronze, the end farthest from the barleycorn flame.

It's warm. It's the warmth of a hand, no more, the same as the Book at her belt has been all afternoon, and for the one heartbeat she gives it, it gets no warmer. With her finger on the plate and the Book beside her knee, she can't tell the two warmths apart. At the far end of the strip the little flame in the carved lantern leans along the bronze toward her finger, the way the bead on the door leaned toward her palm, and stays leaning until she lifts her hand.

She looks at her fingertip in Pell's lantern light. It isn't red and it isn't marked. The scorched glove lies a stride away with its long curved burn across the palm.

The plate lies where it was, on its bare flag, lit.

Sefa gets up and buckles the Book back on. Pell takes the oilskin by its edges and folds the two sheets back into it along their old creases, the letter inside the plan. He tucks the packet inside his jerkin, against his ribs, where no lantern can reach it. He leaves the purse knotted in the bottom of the pack, with the cord, the flask and the shirt beside it, all where Harl Ostley left them.

That's when Bram hears it. It isn't the seam that sighs every few minutes, because this sound doesn't stop. Far down the side stair something crackles, dry and steady, like green wood catching, and it's coming closer. Then light shows on the walls of the stairwell, red-orange, climbing turn by turn. It isn't lamp-yellow.

A little fire comes up the steps. It's knee-high and hunched, embers stacked in the shape of a man, with light running in its seams. It's the first one any of them has seen out of its cracks. It climbs the way the Broom climbs, one step at a time, and stops on the third step below the room's sill. That's where the warm air of the stair meets the dead cool air of the reading room. It sways there and crackles. Its two bright spots don't go to Bram, or to the lantern on the floor. They go past his boots, across the room, to the small yellow pool on the flag by the desk.

It doesn't come up the last three steps, and it doesn't go back down.

It stands a stride and a half from Bram's shield. Behind him are two dry bedrolls, a desk with shelves of real books on it, and ninety feet of library whose every shelf is packed with paper burned soft as ash. Tisk's words come back to him: they know the Broom, and she doesn't know whether they know tall ones.

*(Out of game: the plate is still lying on its flag. Nobody has picked it up yet, so who carries it, and how? Pell's question about the candles is still waiting for Sefa.)*

The fingertip touch is a deliberate, careful act with nothing uncertain in it, so the plate's answer is the world's: warm as a hand, no burn, the flame leaning toward her finger. Both are already in the turn as declared.)*

Sefa reads the letter aloud twice in the dead air of the reading room, flat, charcoal list and all. At *brandy for Corrie's nerves* her voice doesn't change, but she stops for a moment before she goes on.

"Harl Ostley. Corrie. T., who said it bites." She looks at the scorched glove. "Tisk's people burned them proper and never knew their names. I'll give her these before we sleep."

Pell reads the list again under his breath after her, then holds the plan up in the lantern beam so you can both see the nine short arcs, the one inked solid, and the dotted line that runs from this room down and out to the far side of the hatched hall.

"O. Tarr, for Dellmere Iron Factors," Pell says. "They knew which plate to cut, what metal to use, and that it would hurt. Wanted the book whole and *unread*. This wasn't four men stumbling onto treasure." He points to the charcoal list. "And candles specifically not to be lit below. Sefa, does that mean anything to you? Before we try lighting a second lamp."

Sefa lays a finger beside two lines of the clerk's hand without touching the paper. "*Whole and unread.* And *the beast below may be left to itself.* Whoever O. Tarr is, he knew there was a dragon in this hill and sent four men past it for a book he didn't want them to open, and he knew about the plates and the cold iron before any of them had seen the door. That isn't a rumour from the Friendly Arm. Someone has read about this cellar in a room with a desk." She straightens. "Dellmere Iron Factors, Beregost. Half at the Feldepost on delivery. That's a name and a door, and I want it in my pack when we go up. Pell, fold them. The plan too. That dotted line is our proof the four came out at the old door, and it's drawn by someone who's been here or copied someone who was."

"I'd keep these as evidence," Pell says. "Someone at the Feldepost owes Harl and his three an accounting."

Sefa turns to the bronze lying in its own small light on the bare flag. "Now the plate. Bram, hear me out before you say no." Her hands are open at her sides. "It didn't burn the stone for ten days. It didn't burn the oilskin. It burned the man who sawed it off the door and *kept hold of it*. That's not heat; that's refusal. And it leaned for me." A breath. "I'm going to touch it with one fingertip for the length of one heartbeat. Not grip it. If it so much as warms, I take my hand away and we wrap it in the oilskin and carry it like that, and I'll owe you both a very long apology. But if it doesn't, then I know the *no* isn't for me, and I know that before I'm standing in front of the dragon deciding what to do with it. I'd rather learn it here, in a cool room, with Pell's satchel a stride away."

Your eyes go round the camp while they read. Two bedrolls were unrolled and never rolled up again. The lamp was left to burn dry. The heel of bread was saved for later. These were men who planned to be down here a night or two and come back to their beds. The draught from the side stair goes on breathing at your back at its old pace.

Sefa gets up and buckles the Book back on. "Then we go out by the library, under the beam one at a time, and back to the dome. I want to sit with Tisk and give her the names, and I want the three of us to decide tonight, not at his door tomorrow, what *back* is going to mean. He gets the plate in this room in my hands; I said so. What he gets besides, I haven't said." She looks at you. "And Bram, thank you. *Don't pay for another man's cut.* I'll keep that."

Pell takes the oilskin by its edges and folds the two sheets back into it along their old creases, the letter inside the plan. He tucks the packet inside his jerkin, against his ribs. He leaves the purse knotted in the bottom of the pack, with the cord, the flask and the shirt beside it, where they were. "We have the plate and their route. I'm for going back through the library together."

## Round 24

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't shift his feet, but his shield comes up a fraction, not as a threat—as a door. He puts himself fully between the little fire and the room.

"Nobody moves fast," he says, low and even. "It's watching the plate. That's what brought it up. It didn't climb all this way for our boots or our books. Pell, get a bedroll in your hands. If sparks jump toward the shelves, smother them. Sefa, stay where I can see your hands."

He turns his head just enough to speak behind him without losing sight of the stairs.

"Lift the plate again—your hand, not skin. Bring it to the clear space between us and the steps. Slow. Don't wave it. Hold it out where the thing can look at it. If it follows the light, we learn something. If it backs off, better still."

Then, to the creature, calm, as if it might understand or might not:

"Easy, little coal. We're not stealing your warmth. We're taking it back up the long way." He doesn't advance, doesn't lower the shield. "We'll be clear of your house soon."

Sefa Marrin: Sefa doesn't startle. She was a ward-sister; things come through doors at the wrong hour. She lifts one hand toward Bram, palm out, the way she did on the stair for Ixx.

"Don't. Shield stays where it is, hand off the hilt. It's not looking at you." Low and level. "Tisk said they don't talk and they know the Broom. It's come up ninety feet of dead paper to the edge of this room and stopped at the cool air — if it wanted us it had the whole stair to decide. It's looking at *that*." She tips her head at the yellow pool on the flag.

She goes down on one knee beside the plate. Then — since the test was the point of the test — she closes her bare hand around the plain end of the bronze and lifts it. Slowly. Cupped in her palm, lantern-side out, so the barleycorn flame faces the room and not her chest. She holds it as she'd hold a lamp carried across a dark ward: steady, chest-high, no hurry.

"Pell — your question, while I've both hands busy and can't fidget. *Candles not to light below.* The lintel out there says the same thing in older words: *no flame here but the lent one.* I don't think it was about paper. I think whoever briefed Harl knew that an open flame in this hill is a *call* — the house breathes called fire, the little fires are made of it, and a wick is a kin-voice to them. Your lantern's hooded and nothing came. The plate was uncovered and lit full for the first time in ten days — and here one is." She looks at the hunched shape of embers on the third step. "So no, we don't light a second lamp down here from anything but this. And we don't light anything at all near the cracks."

Then she does the thing she wants to do, carefully. She stands, with the plate in her left hand, and turns a quarter so its small light falls across the room and onto the little fire's embers — not a stride closer, just the light. And she watches: whether it leans into the yellow the way the lantern leaned to her palm, or draws back from it, or does nothing at all. *(Watching how it answers the lent light — Insight or passive Perception, whatever fits; I'm not casting anything.)*

"Bram, back toward the slab door, slow, shield to it — you first this time, I'll come last with the light so it can see where the thing it wants is going. Pell, lantern shuttered to a slit; keep the papers against your ribs. If it follows us into the library, it follows at our pace and we let it. If it goes back down the steps —" a breath — "then I think I've learned what it came for, and I'll have one more thing to tell Tisk tonight besides four names."

Quieter, to the Book at her hip, which is warm as a held hand: *Is this yours too? What leaked out under the lid — does it know you?*

Pell Dunmore: “Fire on the steps. It’s watching the plate,” I say quietly. I don’t draw a blade.

“Sefa, lift it with your spell onto a fold of my bedroll. I’ll carry it without skin touching bronze. Bram, keep watching it—we’ll clear the doorway before you back through.”

I lay the folded bedroll beside the plate, clear of its light, ready to receive it. Once it’s placed, I take up the bundle and my lantern and move toward the library door, watching whether the little fire follows the plate’s movement.

“Slowly. No iron, no sudden moves.”
````

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Insight: reading what the little fire wants from the lent light · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Insight: reading what the little fire wants from the lent light · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram doesn't step. His shield comes up two fingers' width and settles square to the mouth of the side stair. He fills the space between the third step and the room the way he'd fill a gap in a wagon line, and says his piece to the thing below him in an even voice. The little fire doesn't answer. Its crackle doesn't change. Its two bright spots never leave the yellow pool on the floor behind his boots, and as far as it's concerned he could be a door.

Sefa lifts her palm toward Bram and tells him what she sees, low and level. Then she goes down on one knee beside the plate and closes her bare hand around its plain end.

The plate is warm the way a hand is warm, the same as it was under her fingertip, and it gets no hotter as her fingers close. The barleycorn flame at the far end leans along the bronze toward the heel of her hand and stays leaning. At her hip the Book holds the same warmth. Her palm doesn't redden. She lifts the plate without hurrying and stands with it cupped at chest height, lantern side out, the way you'd carry a lamp down a ward where people are sleeping.

The thing on the step rises as the plate does. It doesn't climb. It straightens, the hunch going out of its embers, and its spots follow the light up from the floor to the height of her chest. The crackle quickens, like green wood that has finally caught.

While her hands are busy Sefa answers Pell's question about candles, the lintel and the call. Pell has already pulled his bedroll from his pack and folded it. He lays it on the bare flag where the plate was lying, outside its light, ready to take the bronze. It stays empty, because the plate is in Sefa's hand.

Then Sefa turns a quarter where she stands.

The pool of yellow swings across the room. It passes over the dead men's bedrolls, Bram's boots and the iron rim of his shield, and its edge comes to rest on the sill stone, one step above the little fire. That's as far as it reaches.

The thing leans toward it from the waist, the way a man leans toward a hearth on a cold night. Its seams brighten from red to orange. One arm of stacked embers comes up and reaches out over the edge of the second step, and stops a hand's width short of where the yellow lies on the stone. It holds there. It doesn't come up and it doesn't go back down. It sways, and the light runs in its seams.

Sefa watches it the way she'd watch a patient who can't tell her where it hurts.

Sefa can't read it. A patient has a face and this thing has two bright coals where a face would be. Its leaning could be wanting or hunger or simply what fire does near something that burns, and with nothing behind the embers for her to read, she can't tell those apart. What she knows is what anyone in the room can see. It came up ninety feet of stair after the light, it stopped where the light stops, and its reaching arm is still out over the step, a hand's width from the yellow.

The draught breathes up past it, warm and slow. Bram's shield doesn't move. The empty bedroll lies folded on the flag, and the plate burns a quiet yellow in Sefa's bare hand.

*(Out of game: Pell's carry is waiting because the plate is in Sefa's hand, not on his bedroll. Sefa's request that Bram back out first is up to Bram's player.)*

 *(Out of game: none of the three needed a roll, and the last turn already showed each one. Sefa told Bram what she saw. She knelt and lifted the plate bare-handed, and it stayed hand-warm and didn't burn her. She turned so its light fell across the room and stopped at the sill, a step above the little fire.)*
